\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{g}(X_{i})\eta_{i}\big|\leq 2AC_{\mathsf{T}}{\epsilon}_{g}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2}

by Chebyshev’s inequality, where
A=0.1\gamma^{-1/2}
.
Similarly, we also have

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{q}(X_{i})\eta_{i}\big|\leq 2AC_{\mathsf{T}}{\epsilon}_{q}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2},\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{g}(X_{i}){\epsilon}_{i}\big|\leq 2AC_{\mathsf{T}}{\epsilon}_{g}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2}.

From

\mathbb{E}[{\epsilon}_{i}^{2}\eta_{i}^{2}]\leq 4C_{\mathsf{Y}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]

we deduce that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\epsilon}_{i}\eta_{i}\big|\leq 2AC_{\mathsf{Y}}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]^{1/2}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[|\Delta_{g}(X)\Delta_{q}(X)|]\leq{\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q}
by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Chebyshev’s inequality again implies that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{g}(X)\Delta_{q}(X)\big|\leq{\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q}+An^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{2}\Delta_{q}(X)^{2}]^{1/2}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2}.

with a similar reasoning, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{g}(X)^{2}\big|\leq{\epsilon}_{g}^{2}+An^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{4}]^{1/2}\bigg]\geq 1-A^{-2}.

Lastly, since
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{i}^{2}]\geq\delta
by assumption, we also have that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\big|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]\big|\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]\bigg]\geq 1-4n^{-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\eta^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}])^{2}]}{\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]^{2}}\geq 1-8C_{\mathsf{T}}n^{-1}\delta^{-2}\geq 1-0.04\gamma,

so that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]\bigg]\geq 1-0.04\gamma.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all the above high-probability bounds hold, then

\mathbb{P}[{\mathcal{E}}]\geq 1-5A^{-2}-0.04\gamma>1-\gamma.

Under
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]-2AC_{\mathsf{T}}{\epsilon}_{g}n^{-1/2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}],

since
\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]\geq\delta
by assumption and (
42
) implies that
\delta\geq 4ACn^{-1/2}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\bigg|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{q}(X_{i})-{\epsilon}_{i}-\theta_{0}\Delta_{g}(X_{i}))(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq|\theta_{0}|{\epsilon}_{g}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q}+An^{-1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{2}\Delta_{q}(X)^{2}]^{1/2}+|\theta_{0}|\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{4}]^{1/2}+2C_{\mathsf{Y}}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{2}]^{1/2}\big).

It is easy to see that
\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{2}\Delta_{q}(X)^{2}]\leq 4C_{\mathsf{Y}}^{2}{\epsilon}_{g}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{g}(X)^{4}]\leq 4C_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}{\epsilon}_{g}^{2}
. As a result, we can deduce that

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{DML}}-\theta_{0}|

\displaystyle=\left|\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})^{2}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{q}(X_{i})-{\epsilon}_{i}-\theta_{0}\Delta_{g}(X_{i}))(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\delta^{-1}(C_{\theta}{\epsilon}_{g}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q})+8A\big[\delta^{-1}(C_{\mathsf{T}}+C_{\mathsf{Y}}){\epsilon}_{g}+C_{\mathsf{Y}}\delta^{-1/2}\big]n^{-1/2},

concluding the proof.

C.2
Proof of
Theorem

C.2
:
Theorem C.2

(Structure-agnostic upper bound with known treatment noise)
.

Since the variance of
\eta\mid X
is assumed to be
\sigma^{2}
where
\sigma
is a known constant, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{DML}}=\sigma^{-2}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{q}(X_{i})-{\epsilon}_{i}-\theta_{0}\eta_{i})(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})\right),

so that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{DML}}-\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{q}(X_{i})-{\epsilon}_{i})(\Delta_{g}(X_{i})-\eta_{i})-\theta_{0}\Delta_{g}(X_{i})\eta_{i}+\theta_{0}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\right)\right).

Note that the high-probability bounds for each term in the above expression can be obtained with similar arguments as in the previous subsection, with
C=\Theta(\sigma)
and
\delta=\sigma^{2}
. Hence it is straightforward to deduce that

|\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{DML}}-\theta_{0}|\lesssim A\sigma^{-2}{\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q}+AC\big(C_{\theta}\sigma^{-2}{\epsilon}_{g}+\sigma^{-1}\big)n^{-1/2}.

Appendix D
Proof of
Theorem

3.2
:
Theorem 3.2

(The Gaussian treatment barrier)
.

Our proof is based on a constrained risk inequality for testing composite hypothesis developed in
Cai and Low 2011
.

Lemma D.1
.

[
Cai and Low 2011
, Corollary 1]
Let
X
be an observation with distribution
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{i},i=0,1
be two subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}={\mathcal{P}}
, and
\mu_{i}
be some distribution supported on
{\mathcal{P}}
. Define

m_{i}=\int T(P)\mu_{i}(\textup{d}P),\quad v_{i}^{2}=\int\big(T(P)-m_{i}\big)^{2}\mu_{i}(\textup{d}P)

(43)

to be the mean and variance of a functional
T:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
F_{i}
be the distribution of
X
with prior
\mu_{i}
and
f_{i}
be its density with respect to some common dominating measure
\mu
. Then for any estimator
\hat{T}(X)
we have that

\sup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\hat{T}(X)-T(P))^{2}\big]\geq\frac{\big(|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I\big)^{2}}{(I+2)^{2}}

as long as
|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I\geq 0
, where
I=\left(\mathbb{E}_{f_{0}}\left[\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}-1\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}
is the
\chi^{2}
-distance between
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
.

To apply this inequality, we construct the null and alternative hypotheses as mixtures of data distributions with matching moments. While moment matching techniques are widely adopted in establishing minimax lower bounds, the structural nature of our causal model (
1
) brings additional challenges to our construction. Unlike most existing works where only moments of a single variable need to be matched, here we need to match moments that contain two variables: we seek for distributions
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}
over
{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h})
with corresponding mixtures
\bar{P}_{0},\bar{P}_{1}
respectively, such that both

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{0}}\left[(Y-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{0}}[Y\mid X])(T-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{0}}[T\mid X])^{k}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{1}}\left[(Y-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{1}}[Y\mid X])(T-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{1}}[T\mid X])^{k}\right]

and

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{0}}\left[(T-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{0}}[T\mid X])^{k}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{1}}\left[(T-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{P}_{1}}[T\mid X])^{k}\right]

hold for
k=1,2,\cdots,k_{n}
. This would imply that
\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}||\bar{P}_{1})
is small, which further implies that
\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}^{\otimes n}||\bar{P}_{1}^{\otimes n})
is also small, where
\bar{P}_{i}^{\otimes n}:=\int P^{\otimes n}\textup{d}\nu_{i},i=1,2
and
P^{\otimes}
is the
n
-fold product distribution.

To apply
Lemma

D.1
, we need to show that there exists a sufficient gap between
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
, which correspond to the expected value of
\theta
under
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
respectively.
Our key insight is that, for Gaussian treatment, there is no need to match
\mathbb{E}\left[(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X])(T-\mathbb{E}[T\mid X])\right]
since this term always vanishes. This fact is due to a recursive property of the Hermite polynomial
H_{k}(x)=(-1)^{k}\varphi^{(k)}(z)/\varphi(z)
(where
\varphi(\cdot)
is the Gaussian density); we will elaborate on this connection in
Lemma

D.6
. As a result, we can construct mixtures of distributions that are close in terms of
\chi^{2}
-distance (
Corollary

D.1
) but their average values of the
\mathbb{E}\left[(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X])(T-\mathbb{E}[T\mid X])\right]
term are well-separated. Given the structure-agnostic oracle, this separation can be as large as
\tilde{\Omega}({\epsilon}_{g}{\epsilon}_{q})
, and it further induces a separation between
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
at the same scale, yielding the desired lower bound.

In the following, we present the full proof of this theorem.

The following lemma turns out to be a useful tool for moment matching in establishing our lower bounds.

Lemma D.2

(
L_{\infty}
-distance to univariate polynomial bases)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
be a linear space of polynomials on
[-1,1]
in the form of
\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\lambda^{u_{i}}
where
u_{i}\in\{0,1,\cdots,k\}\setminus\{1\}
, and
\delta_{k}
be the
L_{\infty}
-distance of
a(\lambda)=\lambda
to
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
. Then
\delta_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2k^{3}}
.

Proof
:

Let
b(\lambda)\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
be a polynomial that satisfies
\|a-b\|_{L_{\infty}}=\delta_{k}
. Define
r=a-b
, then
r
satisfies
\|r\|_{L_{\infty}}=\delta_{k}
and
r^{\prime}(0)=1
.

Since
\mathrm{deg}(r)\leq k
, the Lagrange interpolation formula implies that

r(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{2k}r(x_{i})\frac{\prod_{j\neq i}(x-x_{j})}{\prod_{j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})}

for any
x_{i}\in[-1,1],1\leq i\leq 2k
.
Taking the derivative of both sides, we obtain

r^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{2k}r(x_{i})\frac{\sum_{l\neq i}\prod_{j\neq i,l}(x-x_{j})}{\prod_{j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})}.

In particular, we choose

x_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} -\frac{k+1-i}{k}\quad&1\leq i\leq k\\
\frac{i-k}{k}\quad&k+1\leq i\leq 2k,\end{aligned}\right.

then it holds that

\left|\frac{\prod_{j\neq i,l}x_{j}}{\prod_{j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\right|=\frac{(k!)^{2}}{ilk^{2k-2}}\frac{ik^{2k-1}}{(k-i)!(k+i)!}=\frac{k}{l}\frac{(k!)^{2}}{(k-i)!(k+i)!}\leq k.

As a result, we have

1=\left|r^{\prime}(0)\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2k}\delta_{k}\sum_{l\neq i}\left|\frac{\prod_{j\neq i,l}x_{j}}{\prod_{j\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\right|\leq 2k^{3}\delta_{k}.

Hence
\delta_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2k^{3}}
as desired.

\square

Lemma D.3

(
L_{\infty}
-distance to bivariate polynomial bases)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}
be a linear space of polynomials on
[-1,1]^{2}
of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\lambda^{u_{i}}\rho^{v_{i}}
where
(u_{i},v_{i})\in\{0,1,\cdots,k\}\times\{0,1\}\setminus\{(1,1)\}
, and
\delta_{k,1}
be the
L_{\infty}
-distance from
a_{1}(\lambda,\rho)=\lambda\rho
to
\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}
. Then
\delta_{k,1}\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}
.

Proof
:

Assume the contrary holds,
i.e.

\delta_{k,1}<\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}
, then there exists some
b_{1}(\lambda,\rho)\in\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}
such that
\|b_{1}-a_{1}\|_{L_{\infty}}<\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}
. By definition, there exists polynomials
r_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
and
s_{1}
such that
b_{1}(\lambda,\rho)=\rho r_{1}(\lambda)+s_{1}(\lambda)
. In particular, setting
\rho=1
and
\rho=-1
implies that
\|r_{1}+s_{1}-\lambda\|_{L_{\infty}}<\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}
and
\|r_{1}-s_{1}-\lambda\|_{L_{\infty}}<\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}
. The triangle inequality implies that
\|r_{1}-\lambda\|_{L_{\infty}}<\delta_{k}
, which is a contradiction to the definition of
\delta_{k}
. Thus the conclusion follows.

\square

Lemma D.4

(Separation of measures under matching properties)
.

There exists two probability measures
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
on
[-1,1]^{2}
such that

\int a(\lambda,\rho)\nu_{0}(\textup{d}\lambda\textup{d}\rho)=\int a(\lambda,\rho)\nu_{1}(\textup{d}\lambda\textup{d}\rho),\quad\forall a\in\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}

(44)

and

\int\lambda\rho\nu_{0}(\textup{d}\lambda\textup{d}\rho)-\int\lambda\rho\nu_{1}(\textup{d}\lambda\textup{d}\rho)\geq\frac{1}{4k^{3}}.

(45)

Proof
:

The proof is similar to that of
[
Cai and Low 2011
, Lemma 1]
. Let
C([-1,1]^{2})
be the space of continuous functions on
[-1,1]^{2}
equipped with the
L_{\infty}
norm and
{\mathcal{F}}
be linear space spanned by
a_{1}(\lambda,\rho)=\lambda\rho
and
\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}
. Define a linear functional
T
that maps any
f=ca_{1}+g\in{\mathcal{F}}
(where
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
) to
c\delta_{k,1}
, where
\delta_{k,1}
is defined in the previous lemma. Let
g_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
be the best
L_{\infty}
-approximation of
a_{1}
in
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
, then
\|a_{1}-g_{1}\|_{L_{\infty}}=\delta_{k,1}
and
T(a_{1}-g_{1})=\delta_{k,1}
, so
\|T\|\geq 1
. On the other hand, for any
f=ca_{1}+g\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we have
\|f\|_{\infty}\geq|c|\delta_{k,1}
since otherwise the
L_{\infty}
distance between
a_{1}
and
-c^{-1}g
would be smaller than
\delta_{k,1}
, which is a contradiction. Thus
|T(f)|=|c|\delta_{k,1}\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}
, which implies that
\|T\|\leq 1
.

Therefore, we must have
\|T\|=1
. By Hahn-Banach theorem,
T
can be extended to a linear functional on
C([-1,1]^{2})
with unit norm, which we still denote by
T
. The Riesz representer theorem then implies that there exists a signed measure
\mu
with unit total variation such that
T(f)=\int f\textup{d}\mu,\forall f\in C([-1,1]^{2})
. In particular, we have

\int a(\lambda,\rho)\textup{d}\mu=0,\forall a\in\mathfrak{P}_{k,1}\quad\text{and}\quad\int\lambda\rho\textup{d}\mu=\delta_{k,1}.

Finally, by the Hahn decomposition theorem, there exists (positive) measures
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}
such that
\mu=\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}
. Then it is easy to see that such
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
satisfiy the desired properties, concluding the proof.

\square

In the following, we provide the full proof of
Theorem

3.2
. For any
A>0
and
q\in(0,1)
, we define a
two-piece Bernoulli distribution
, denoted by
\mathrm{B}_{2}(q;A)
, as a distribution with PDF

p(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &A^{-1}q&\quad x\in[0,A]\\
&A^{-1}(1-q)&\quad x\in[-A,0)\\
&0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

It is easy to see that such a distribution has mean
\left(q-\frac{1}{2}\right)A
.

To begin with, note that we can assume that
|\hat{g}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{T}}-{\epsilon}_{1}/2
and
|\hat{q}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{q}}-{\epsilon}_{2}/2
without loss of generality. Indeed, since
|\hat{g}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{T}}
and
|\hat{q}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{q}}
, there exists
\tilde{h}=(\tilde{g}(\cdot),\tilde{q}(\cdot))
satisfying
\|\hat{q}-\tilde{q}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq{\epsilon}_{1}/2,\|\hat{g}-\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq{\epsilon}_{2}/2
and
|\tilde{g}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{T}}-{\epsilon}_{1}/2,|\tilde{q}(X)|\leq C_{\mathsf{q}}-{\epsilon}_{2}/2
. Then,
{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}/2}(\tilde{h})\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h})
, and any lower bound for
{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}/2}(\tilde{h})
also applies to
{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h})
, implying the desired lower bound up to constants. In the following, we will replace
\hat{h}
with
\tilde{h}
work with the uncertainty set
{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}/2}(\hat{h})
.

Now, let
A=C_{\mathsf{q}},Q=C_{\mathsf{q}}-{\epsilon}_{2}/2
and
G=C_{\mathsf{T}}-{\epsilon}_{1}/2
. Let
k_{n}>0
be some even integer that will be specified later, and
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}
be the corresponding distributions in
Lemma

D.4
. For any
\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the following data generating process:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\sim P_{X}=\mathrm{Uniform}({\mathcal{X}})

(46)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\sim{\mathcal{N}}\big(g_{\lambda}(X),\sigma^{2}\big)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\sim\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{B}_{2}(q;4A),q=\frac{1}{4A}\left(2A+\theta_{\lambda,\rho}T+f_{\lambda,\rho}(X)\right)&\quad\text{if }\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}T+f_{\lambda,\rho}(X)\right|\leq A\\
&{\mathcal{N}}\left(\theta_{\lambda,\rho}T+f_{\lambda,\rho}(X),1\right)&\quad\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

where

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\hat{g}(x)+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\sigma\lambda

\displaystyle q_{\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=\hat{q}(x)-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho

\displaystyle\theta_{\lambda,\rho}

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\sigma^{-1}\lambda\rho

\displaystyle f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=q_{\rho}(x)-\theta_{\lambda,\rho}g_{\lambda}(x).

By choosing
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}=\sigma^{-1}{\epsilon}_{1}/4
and
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}={\epsilon}_{2}/4
, we can ensure that for any
h_{\lambda}=(g_{\lambda},q_{\lambda})
it holds that
h_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}/2}(\hat{h}),\forall s\in[1,+\infty]^{2}
.

We use
P_{\lambda,\rho}
to denote the joint distribution of
(X,T,Y)
in (
46
), and
p_{\lambda,\rho}
be its density. Then we have that

p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi\left(\frac{t-g_{\lambda}(x)}{\sigma}\right)\cdot\left(2A+\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right)&\text{ if }\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|\leq A\text{ and }y\in[0,2A]\\
&\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi\left(\frac{t-g_{\lambda}(x)}{\sigma}\right)\cdot\left(2A-\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t-f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right)&\text{ if }\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|\leq A\text{ and }y\in[-2A,0)\\
&0&\text{ if }\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|\leq A\text{ and }|y|>A\\
&\varphi\left(\frac{t-g_{\lambda}(x)}{\sigma}\right)\varphi\left(y-\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t-f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right)&\text{ if }\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|>A.\end{aligned}\right.

(47)

The following lemma derives an equivalent expression for
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,T=t]=\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)
:

Lemma D.5

(Expression for conditional mean outcome)
.

For any
x,t
we have

\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)=\hat{q}(x)+\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda\rho(t-\hat{g}(x))-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho-\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda^{2}\rho.

Note that the last event in (
47
) happens with small probability. Indeed, we can define a
good
event

{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}=\left\{|T|\leq\frac{A-Q-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\max\{1,\sigma\}^{2}}-G-1\right\}.

(48)

An important property of the above definition is that the bound goes to infinity since we assumed that
{\epsilon}_{1}=o(1)
and
{\epsilon}_{2}=o(1)
, so that it would happen with high probability.

The following result summarizes the good properties enjoyed by
{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
:

Proposition D.1

(Properties of good events)
.

We have

1.

\lim_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\to 0}\inf_{|\lambda|,|\rho|\leq 1}P_{\lambda,\rho}[{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}]=1
;

2.

If
t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
, then for any
\lambda,\rho\in[-1,1]
and any
x
, we have
\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|\leq A
.

Proof
:

By definition,
P_{\lambda,\rho}[{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}]=P_{T\sim{\mathcal{N}}(g_{\lambda}(x),\sigma^{2})}\left[|T|\leq\frac{A-Q-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\max\{1,\sigma\}^{2}}-G-1\right]
. Since
|g_{\lambda}(x)|\leq G+o(1)
, (1) directly follows. To prove (2), it suffices to note that

\displaystyle\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}T+f_{\lambda,\rho}(X)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\hat{q}(X)+\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda\rho(T-\hat{g}(X))-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho-\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda^{2}\rho\right|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\max\{1,\sigma\}^{2}\left(|T|+G+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\right)+Q+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}

where the first equation is due to
Lemma

D.5
.

\square

Let
H_{k}
be the
k
-th order Hermite polynomial and
\bar{P}_{0}
and
\bar{P}_{1}
be the mixture of
P_{\lambda,\rho}
with priors
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
respectively, and
\hat{p}_{0}
and
\bar{p}_{1}
be their densities. Our next step would be to bound the
\chi^{2}
-divergence between
\bar{P}_{0}
and
\bar{P}_{1}
. To do this, we need to analyze the densities
\bar{p}_{i}(x,t,y),i=0,1
for two cases
(x,t,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
and
(x,t,y)\notin{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
separately. Our next two lemmas handle the first case.

Lemma D.6

(Taylor expansions of perturbed densities)
.

Suppose that
t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
, then we have

p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi(z)\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}\left(2A+\hat{q}(x)+(k-1)\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\right)H_{k}(z)&\text{if }y\in[0,A]\\
&\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi(z)\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}\left(2A-\hat{q}(x)-(k-1)\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\right)H_{k}(z)&\text{if }y\in[-A,0)\end{aligned}\right.

where
z=\frac{t-\hat{g}(x)}{\sigma}
.

Proof
:

We only prove the statement for
y\in[-A,0)
; the other case
y\in[0,A]
can be handled similarly.
Since

\varphi\left(\frac{t-g_{\lambda}(x)}{\sigma}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}H_{k}\left(z\right)\varphi\left(z\right)\frac{(-1)^{k}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}

and

2A-\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t-f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)=2A-\hat{q}(x)-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\left[1-\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\lambda(t-\hat{g}(x))+\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right]

by
Lemma

D.5
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi\left(z\right)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}H_{k}\left(z\right)\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}\right)\left[2A-\hat{q}(x)-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\left(1-\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\lambda(t-\hat{g}(x))+\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\lambda^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi\left(z\right)\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}\left[(2A-\hat{q}(x))H_{k}(z)-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\left(H_{k}(z)-kzH_{k-1}(z)+(k-1)kH_{k-2}(z)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi\left(z\right)\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\lambda^{k}}{k!\sigma^{k}}\left(2A-\hat{q}(x)-(k-1)\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho\right)H_{k}(z),

where in the final step holds since
H_{k}(z)=zH_{k-1}(z)-(k-1)H_{k-2}(z)
. The conclusion follows.

\square

Lemma D.7

(Bounding
\chi^{2}
-distance under good event)
.

We have

\int\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\mathbbm{1}\left\{t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\textup{d}x\textup{d}t\textup{d}y\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}.

Proof
:

Let
(x,t,y)
satisfies
t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
and
y\in[-A,0)
, then
Lemma

D.6
implies that

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi(z)\bigg[(2A-\hat{q}(x))\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}H_{k}(z)\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}}{k!}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\textup{d}\big(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}\big)

(49)

\displaystyle-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(z)\frac{(k-1)\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}}{k!}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\rho\textup{d}\big(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi(z)\bigg[(2A-\hat{q}(x))\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}H_{k}(z)\frac{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}}{k!}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\textup{d}\big(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}\big)

\displaystyle-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{\infty}H_{k}(z)\frac{(k-1)\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}}{k!}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\rho\textup{d}\big(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A^{2}}\varphi(z)\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}H_{k}(z)c_{k}(x),

where
z=\frac{t-\hat{g}(x)}{\sigma}
and

c_{k}(x)=k^{-1}(2A-\hat{q}(x))\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\textup{d}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\int_{[-1,1]}\lambda^{k}\rho\textup{d}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}).

(50)

On the other hand, since
x\mapsto e^{-x}
is convex, by Jensen’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)

\displaystyle=\int p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)\textup{d}\nu_{0}(\lambda,\rho)

(51)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4A}\int\varphi(z-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\lambda)\textup{d}\nu_{0}(\lambda,\rho)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4A}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int\left(z-\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\lambda\right)^{2}\textup{d}\nu_{0}(\lambda,\rho)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4A}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}z^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{8A}\varphi(z)

for sufficiently large
n
, since
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}=o(1)
. Hence,

\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\leq\frac{1}{2A^{3}}\varphi(z)\left(\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}H_{k}(z)c_{k}(x)\right)^{2}.

Fixing
x,y
and integrating both sides of (
49
) with respect to
t
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\int\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\mathbbm{1}\left\{t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\textup{d}t

(52)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2A^{3}}\int\varphi(z)\left(\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}H_{k}(z)c_{k}(x)\right)^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\sigma z+\hat{g}(x)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\cdot\sigma\textup{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma}{2A^{3}}\int\varphi(z)\left(\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}H_{k}(z)c_{k}(x)\right)^{2}\textup{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma}{2A^{3}}\left[\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{c_{k}^{2}(x)}{((k-1)!)^{2}}\int\varphi(z)H_{k}^{2}(z)\textup{d}z+2\sum_{j>i\geq k_{n}+1}\frac{c_{i}(x)c_{j}(x)}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\int\varphi(z)H_{i}(z)H_{j}(z)\textup{d}z\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma}{2A^{3}}\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{kc_{k}^{2}(x)}{(k-1)!}\leq\frac{\sigma}{A^{3}}\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{+\infty}\frac{c_{k}^{2}(x)}{(k-2)!},

where the last step follows from the orthogonality of Hermite polynomials:

\int\varphi(z)H_{k}^{2}(z)=k!\quad\text{and}\quad\int\varphi(z)H_{i}(z)H_{j}(z)\textup{d}z=0,\forall i\neq j.

Moreover, (
50
) implies that

|c_{k}(x)|\leq 6Ak^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}+2\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\leq 8A\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{k}.

Plugging into (
52
), we can deduce that

\int\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\mathbbm{1}\left\{t\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\textup{d}t\leq 64\sigma A^{-1}\sum_{k=k_{n}-1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2k}\leq 100\sigma A^{-1}\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}.

For
t\in[0,A]
, the above inequality can be established in a similar fashion. As a result, we have

\int\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\textup{d}x\textup{d}t\textup{d}y\leq 100\sigma A^{-1}\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}\int\textup{d}x\textup{d}y\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1},

as desired.

\square

Our next lemma, on the other hand, develops bounds for densities outside
{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
. It essentially shows that this part makes a negligible contribution to the overall
\chi^{2}
-divergence.

Lemma D.8

(Bounding
\chi^{2}
-distance under bad event)
.

For any
t\notin{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
, we have

\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}\leq 16\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right).

Proof
:

For any
t\notin{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}
and any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, define

\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(0)}:=\left\{(\lambda,\rho)\in[-1,1]^{2}\mid\left|\theta_{\lambda,\rho}t+f_{\lambda,\rho}(x)\right|\leq A\right\}

and

\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(1)}:=[-1,1]^{2}\setminus\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(0)}.

For any
(\lambda,\rho),(\hat{\lambda},\hat{\rho})\in[-1,1]^{2}
such that
(\lambda,\rho),(\hat{\lambda}_{i},\hat{\rho}_{i}^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad-\log p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)

(53a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\big(t-g_{\lambda}(x)\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left[y-\hat{q}(x)-\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda_{i}\rho_{i}(t-\hat{g}(x))+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\rho+\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda_{i}^{2}\rho_{i}\right]^{2}

(53b)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\big(t-g_{\lambda}(x)\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left[y-\hat{q}(x)-\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\rho}_{i}(t-\hat{g}(x))+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\rho}_{i}+\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{2}\hat{\rho}_{i}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}^{2}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}

(53c)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{3\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left[y-\hat{q}(x)-\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{\rho}_{i}(t-\hat{g}(x))+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\rho}_{i}+\sigma^{-2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{2}\hat{\rho}_{i}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}+\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}^{2}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}-\frac{4\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}

(53d)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\log p_{\hat{\lambda},\hat{\rho}}(x,t,y)+\frac{1}{24\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2},

(53e)

where (
53b
) follows from (
47
), (
53c
) and (
53d
) follow from the inequality
u^{2}\geq\rho(u+v)^{2}-\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}v^{2},\forall\rho\in(0,1)
, and (
53e
) holds because
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}=o(1)
and
|t-\hat{g}(x)|=\Omega(1)
by (
48
). With a similar reasoning, we can also deduce from (
53b
) that

-\log p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)\geq\frac{1}{4\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2},\quad\forall(\lambda,\rho)\text{ s.t. }(\lambda,\rho)\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(1)}.

(54)

Define

I_{i,j}=\int_{(\lambda,\rho)\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(i)}}p_{\lambda}(x,t,y)\textup{d}\nu_{j}(\lambda,\rho),\quad i,j\in\{0,1\},

where we drop the dependency on
(x,t,y)
for convenience.
Then from (
53
) we can deduce that

\displaystyle I_{1,1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{(\lambda,\rho)\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(1)}}p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)^{2}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)\min_{(\lambda,\rho)\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(1)}}p_{\lambda,\rho}(x,t,y)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)I_{1,0}.

Thus, combining the above inequality (
54
) we have

\frac{(I_{1,0}-I_{1,1})^{2}}{I_{1,0}}\leq 2\left(I_{1,0}+I_{1,0}^{-1}I_{1,1}^{2}\right)\leq 4\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right).

(55)

On the other hand, from (
47
) it is easy to see that for any
(\lambda,\rho)\in\Lambda_{x,t,y}^{(0)}
we have

\frac{1}{4A}\varphi(z)\leq p_{\lambda}(x,t,y)\leq\frac{3}{4A}\varphi(z)

where
z=\frac{t-g_{\lambda}(x)}{\sigma}
, so
\frac{1}{3}I_{0,0}\leq I_{0,1}\leq 3I_{0,0}
and

\frac{(I_{0,0}-I_{0,1})^{2}}{I_{0,0}}\leq 4I_{0,0}\leq 4A^{-1}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right),

(56)

where the last inequality is due to the bound
I_{0,0}\leq A^{-1}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)
, which directly follows from its definition (
47
) and the argument used in (
53e
).

Combining (
55
) and (
56
), we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(I_{0,0}-I_{0,1}+I_{1,0}-I_{1,1})^{2}}{I_{0,0}+I_{1,0}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{(I_{0,0}-I_{0,1})^{2}}{I_{0,0}}+\frac{(I_{1,0}-I_{1,1})^{2}}{I_{1,0}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 16\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right),

as desired.

\square

Combining the results of
Lemma

D.7
and
Lemma

D.8
, we obtain the following:

Corollary D.1

(Bounding the whole
\chi^{2}
-distance between the null and alternative distributions)
.

Let
\bar{P}_{0}
and
\bar{P}_{1}
be the mixture distributions as defined before. Then we have that

\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}||\bar{P}_{1})\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}+96A^{-1}\sigma^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\exp\left(-\frac{A^{2}}{24\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}^{2}}\right).

(57)

In particular, if
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}=o\left(\log^{-1/2}n\right)
and
k_{n}\geq-\frac{\log n}{\log\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}}
, then
\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}||\bar{P}_{1})=o\left((nk_{n}^{3})^{-1}\right)
and
\chi^{2}\left(\bar{P}_{0}^{\otimes n}||\bar{P}_{1}^{\otimes n}\right)=o\left(k_{n}^{-3}\right)
.

Proof
:

By definition we have

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}||\bar{P}_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}}\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)^{2}}\textup{d}\bar{P}_{0}+\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}^{c}}\frac{\left(\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)-\bar{p}_{1}(x,t,y)\right)^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x,t,y)^{2}}\textup{d}\bar{P}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}+16\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1},\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}^{c}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)\textup{d}x\textup{d}t\textup{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}+16\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12\sigma^{2}}\big(t-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}\left\{|t-\hat{g}(x)|\geq\frac{A}{2\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\textup{d}x\textup{d}t

\displaystyle=200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}+16\sqrt{6}\sigma\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\big(s-\hat{g}(x)\big)^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}\left\{|s-\hat{g}(x)|\geq\frac{A}{2\sqrt{6}\sigma\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}}\right\}\textup{d}x\textup{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 200\sigma\left(\frac{e\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}}{k_{n}-1}\right)^{k_{n}-1}+96A^{-1}\sigma^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\exp\left(-\frac{A^{2}}{24\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}^{2}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}^{2}}\right).

If
\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}=o\left(\log^{1/2}n\right)
and
k_{n}
is the smallest integer satisfying
k_{n}\geq-\frac{\log n}{\log\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}}
, it is easy to see that both terms in (
57
) are
o\left((nk_{n}^{3})^{-1}\right)
, so
\chi^{2}(\bar{P}_{0}||\bar{P}_{1})=o\left((nk_{n}^{3})^{-1}\right)
. It follows that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}\left(\bar{P}_{0}^{\otimes n}||\bar{P}_{1}^{\otimes n}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{p}_{0}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{p}_{1}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})\right)^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{p}_{0}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})}\textup{d}x_{1}\cdots\textup{d}x_{n}\textup{d}t_{1}\cdots\textup{d}t_{n}\textup{d}y_{1}\cdots\textup{d}y_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{p}_{1}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})\right)^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{p}_{0}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})}\textup{d}x_{1}\cdots\textup{d}x_{n}\textup{d}t_{1}\cdots\textup{d}t_{n}\textup{d}y_{1}\cdots\textup{d}y_{n}-1

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int\frac{\bar{p}_{1}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})^{2}}{\bar{p}_{0}(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})}\textup{d}x_{i}\textup{d}t_{i}\textup{d}y_{i}-1

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+o\left((nk_{n}^{3})^{-1}\right)\right)^{n}-1=o\left(k_{n}^{-3}\right),

which concludes the proof.

\square

Finally, we can apply
Lemma

D.1
to deduce our lower bound. We define the following functional
T
: for any observation distribution
P_{\lambda,\rho}^{\otimes n}
of
\{(x_{i},t_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
generated from a model in (
46
),
T(P)
equals the corresponding parameter value
\theta_{\lambda,\rho}
. Let
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}
be the distributions that satisfy the property in
Lemma

D.4
corresponding to the
k_{n}
in
Corollary

D.1
; we can also view
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
as distributions on the
P_{\lambda,\rho}^{\otimes n}
’s. Note that
\theta_{\lambda,\rho}=\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\lambda\rho
by (
46
), we know from
Lemma

D.4
that the mean difference between
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
is

m_{1}-m_{0}=\int T\left(P_{\lambda,\rho}^{\otimes n}\right)\textup{d}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})(\lambda,\rho)=\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\int\lambda\rho\textup{d}(\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})(\lambda,\rho)\geq\frac{1}{4\sigma k_{n}^{3}}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}.

On the other hand, we clearly have
v_{0}\leq 2\sigma^{-1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}
, and
Corollary

D.1
implies that
I=\chi^{2}\left(\bar{P}_{0}^{\otimes n}||\bar{P}_{1}^{\otimes n}\right)=o\left(k_{n}^{-3}\right)
. So for sufficiently large
n
, we have
m_{1}-m_{0}-v_{0}I\geq\frac{1}{8\sigma k_{n}^{3}}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}
. By
Lemma

D.1
, the minimax mean-square error for any estimator
\hat{T}
is at least

\Omega\left(k_{n}^{-3}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\right)=\Omega\left(-\left(\frac{\log n}{\log\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}}\right)^{-3}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{1}\tilde{{\epsilon}}_{2}\right)=\Omega\left(-\left(\frac{\log n}{\log{\epsilon}_{n,g}}\right)^{-3}\sigma^{-2}{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}\right).

In other words, we have

\mathfrak{M}_{n,1-\gamma}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h})\right)\geq-c_{\gamma}\left(\frac{\log n}{\log{\epsilon}_{1}}\right)^{-3}\sigma^{-2}{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}.

(58)

It remains to prove the
n^{-1/2}
component of the lower bound. Our proof relies on the following lemma that derives the
\chi^{2}
-divergence between two Gaussian mixtures.

Lemma D.9

(
\chi^{2}
-distance for a specific Gaussian model)
.

Let
P_{i},i=0,1
be the distribution of
(X,T,Y)
generated from

X\sim P_{X},\quad T\mid X\sim{\mathcal{N}}(g_{i}(X),\sigma^{2}),\quad Y|X,T\sim{\mathcal{N}}(q_{i}(X)+(T-g_{i}(X))\theta_{i},1),

(59)

such that
\sqrt{2}\sigma|\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}|<1
. Then

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{1},P_{0})

\displaystyle=\big[1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}\big]^{-1/2}\int\exp\left(\frac{\big[q_{1}(x)-q_{0}(x)+(\theta_{1}-2\theta_{0})(g_{1}(x)-g_{0}(x))\big]^{2}}{1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}}\right)\textup{d}x-1.

Proof
:

It is easy to see that the density of
P_{i}
can be written as

p_{i}(x,t,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma}p_{X}(x)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(t-g_{i}(x))^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big(y-q_{i}(x)-(t-g_{i}(x))\theta_{i}\big)^{2}\right),

thus

\displaystyle-2\log p_{1}(x,t,y)+\log p_{0}(x,t,y)

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\frac{p_{X}(x)}{2\pi\sigma}\right)+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}-\big[2\big(q_{1}(x)+(t-g_{1}(x))\theta_{1}\big)-\big(q_{0}(x)+(t-g_{0}(x))\theta_{0}\big)\big]y

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\big[2(t-g_{1}(x))^{2}-(t-g_{0}(x))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\big[2(q_{1}(x)+(t-g_{1}(x))\theta_{1})^{2}-(q_{0}(x)+(t-g_{0}(x))\theta_{0})^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\frac{p_{X}(x)}{2\pi\sigma}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left[y-\big(2\big(q_{1}(x)+(t-g_{1}(x))\theta_{1}\big)+\big(q_{0}(x)+(t-g_{0}(x))\theta_{0}\big)\big)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\big[2(t-g_{1}(x))^{2}-(t-g_{0}(x))^{2}\big]-\big[\big(q_{1}(x)+(t-g_{1}(x))\theta_{1}\big)-\big(q_{0}(x)+(t-g_{0}(x))\theta_{0}\big)\big]^{2}

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\frac{p_{X}(x)}{2\pi\sigma}\right)+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\left[y-\big(2\big(q_{1}(x)+(t-g_{1}(x))\theta_{1}\big)+\big(q_{0}(x)+(t-g_{0}(x))\theta_{0}\big)\big)\right]^{2}}_{:=a(y,t,x)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left[\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}-(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}\right](t-c(x))^{2}}_{:=b(t,x)}-\underbrace{\frac{\big[q_{1}(x)-q_{0}(x)+(\theta_{1}-2\theta_{0})(g_{1}(x)-g_{0}(x))\big]^{2}}{1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}}}_{:=d(x)}

where
c(x)
is some irrelevant function of
x
. By taking integration, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{1},P_{0})=\int\frac{p_{1}^{2}(x,t,y)}{p_{0}(x,t,y)}\textup{d}x\textup{d}t\textup{d}y-1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma}\int p_{X}(x)\exp(-a(y,t,x)-b(t,x)+d(x))\textup{d}x\textup{d}t\textup{d}y-1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma}\int p_{X}(x)\exp(d(x))\textup{d}x\int\exp(-b(t,x))\textup{d}t\int\exp(-a(y,t,x))\textup{d}y

\displaystyle=\big[1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}\big]^{-1/2}\int\exp\left(d(x)\right)\textup{d}x-1

as desired.

\square

The next corollary highlights the special case of
Lemma

D.9
that we will use in our proof:

Corollary D.2

(Bounding the
\chi^{2}
-distance)
.

In the setting of
Lemma

D.9
, if
g_{0}=g_{1}
,
q_{0}=q_{1}
and
\sigma|\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}|\leq 0.1
, then
\chi^{2}(P_{1},P_{0})\leq 2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}
.

Proof
:

By
Lemma

D.9
, we have
\chi^{2}(P_{1},P_{0})\leq\big[1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}\big]^{-1/2}-1
, and
\sigma|\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}|\leq 0.1
implies that

\big[1-2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2}\big]^{-1/2}\leq 1+2\sigma^{2}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})^{2},

concluding the proof.

\square

We now define

\hat{\theta}=0,\quad\tilde{\theta}=\xi^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}n^{-1/2}/2

and let
\hat{P}
and
\tilde{P}
be distributions of
(X,T,Y)
generated from (
59
) with
(g,q,\theta)=(\hat{g},\hat{q},\hat{\theta})
and
(\hat{g},\hat{q},\tilde{\theta})
respectively. Then
Corollary

D.2
implies that
\chi^{2}(\tilde{P},\hat{P})\leq\xi n^{-1}/2
and thus

H(\tilde{P}^{\otimes n},\hat{P}^{\otimes n})\leq nH(\tilde{P},\hat{P})\leq n\chi^{2}(\tilde{P},\hat{P})\leq\xi/2.

Therefore,
Lemma

A.1
implies that for any estimator
\hat{T}
, it holds that

\sup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}P\left[\left|\hat{T}-T(P)\right|\geq\xi^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}n^{-1/2}/4\right]\geq\frac{1-\sqrt{\xi(1-\xi/4)}}{2}=\gamma.

Equivalently, we have

\mathfrak{M}_{1-\gamma}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h})\right)\geq\xi^{1/2}\sigma^{-1}n^{-1/2}/4.

(60)

Combining (
58
) and (
60
), we obtain the desired result.

Appendix E
General upper bounds under Neyman Orthogonality

E.1
Estimation error of general moment estimators

In this section, we establish upper bounds for general orthogonal estimators beyond DML.

To state our first result, we require several assumptions, as stated below. These assumptions largely follows
[
Mackey et al. 2018
]
. We first define the Neyman orthogonality property of moment functions.

Definition E.1

(Orthogonality of moment function)
.

A moment function
m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X)):\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
(S_{0},S_{1})
-orthogonal for some sets
S_{1}\subseteq S_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}
, if for any
\alpha\in S_{0}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\mid X\right]=0
a.s., and for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in S_{1}
, we have
D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},\gamma)=0
a.s., where
D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},\gamma):=\nabla_{\gamma_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla_{\gamma_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\nabla_{\gamma_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}m(Z,\theta_{0},\gamma),\forall\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.

This property is the key to constructing efficient structure-agnostic estimators.

Assumption E.1

(Main assumptions)
.

Let
S_{1}\subseteq S_{0}
be non-empty sets and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, then the following conditions hold:

1.

The moment
m
is
(S_{0},S_{1})
-orthogonal.

2.

\mathbb{E}_{P}[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))]\neq 0
for all
\theta\neq\theta_{0}
.

3.

\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}[\nabla_{\theta}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))]\big|\geq\delta_{\mathsf{id}}
and
\mathrm{Var}_{P}\left(m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right)\leq V_{\mathsf{m}}
.

4.

D^{\alpha}m
exists and is continuous for all
\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k+1
.

The specific choices of
S_{0},S_{1}
and
k
will be explicitly stated in all our results. In
E.1
, (1) requires orthogonality of the moment function, (2) guarantees that
\theta_{0}
is the unique solution to the moment equation, (3) guarantees identifiability of
\theta_{0}
, and lastly, (4) requires sufficient regularity of the moment function. Finally, we assume the following regularity conditions:

Assumption E.2

(Additional regularity assumptions)
.

Define
{\mathcal{B}}_{h_{0},r}=\left\{h\in{\mathcal{H}}:\max_{\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}|h_{i}(X)-h_{0,i}(X)|^{2\alpha_{i}}\right]\leq r\right\}
. Then there exists
r>0
such that

1.

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|\theta-\theta_{0}|\leq r}\left\|\nabla_{\theta}m_{\theta}(Z,\theta,h_{0}(X))\right\|\right]<+\infty
;

2.

For any compact set
A\subseteq\Theta
, it holds that
\sup_{\theta\in A,h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{h_{0},r}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla_{\gamma}m(Z,\theta,h(X))\|^{2}\right]<+\infty
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in A,h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{h_{0},r}}\left|m(Z,\theta,h(X))\right|\right]<+\infty
;

3.

\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{h_{0},r}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|\theta-\theta_{0}|\leq r}\|\nabla_{\theta,\gamma}m(Z,\theta,h(X))\|^{2}\right]<+\infty
;

4.

\lambda_{\star}(\theta_{0},h_{0}):=\max_{\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k+1}\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{h_{0},r}}\|D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta,h(X))\|_{2p}<+\infty
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}
the the set of all indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}
such that
\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell,0}={\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{k-1,\ell}
.
The following theorem shows that orthogonal moments as in
Definition

E.1
directly yields efficient structure-agnostic estimators of
\theta_{0}
.

Theorem E.1

(Structure-agnostic guarantee for general orthogonal estimators)
.

Let
S_{1}\subseteq S_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell},k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
p,q\in[1,+\infty]
be such that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be a set of distributions of
(X,T,Y)
generated from (
1
),
{\epsilon}_{i}>0,i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
and
s\geq q\max_{\alpha\in S}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}
. Further let
\Phi
be an arbitrary mapping that maps
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
to some function
h_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
in some vector space
{\mathcal{F}}
. Consider the estimate
\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}}
obtained by solving the moment equations

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m\left(Z_{i},\theta,\hat{h}\right)=0.

(61)

Suppose that the moment function
m:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
E.1
with
S_{0},S_{1},k
specified above and additional regularity conditions (stated in
E.2
) for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. then for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))

\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\times\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{V_{\mathsf{m}}}{n}}+\lambda_{\star}\sum_{\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0})\cup({\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1})}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\bigg)

(62)

with probability
\geq 1-\gamma
, where

\lambda_{\star}=\sup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\max_{\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0})\cup({\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1})}\left\|D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right\|_{L^{p}(P)}.

Additionally, when the nuisance error rates are sufficiently fast, we have the following asymptotic normality guarantee for
\hat{\theta}
:

Corollary E.1

(Asymptotic normality)
.

Suppose that
\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}=o(n^{-1/2})
for all
\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0})\cup({\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1})
, then
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})\xrightarrow{d}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-2}V_{\mathsf{m}})
.

The proof can be found in
Section

E.3
.

E.2
Proofs of
Theorem

E.1
and
Corollary

E.1

The proof is based on the standard arguments for bouding estimation errors of orthogonal estimators; see
e.g.

[
Mackey et al. 2018
, Section A]
. The only major difference is that our bound is structure-agnostic while their goal is to establish
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
convergence rate under assumptions on nuisance errors. For conciseness, we will not repeat the arguments that have already been covered in their paper.

To begin with, their eq.(10) shows that

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})\mathbbm{1}\{\det J(\hat{h})\neq 0\}=J(\hat{h})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}\{\det J(\hat{h})\neq 0\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X_{i})),

where
J(\hat{h})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{\theta}^{\prime}(Z_{i},\tilde{\theta},\hat{h}(X_{i}))
for some
\theta=\lambda\theta_{0}+(1-\lambda)\hat{\theta},\lambda\in[0,1]
and
J=\mathbb{E}[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))]
. They also show that
J(\hat{h})^{-1}\mathbb{I}[\operatorname{det}J(\hat{h})\neq 0]\xrightarrow{p}J^{-1}
. Hence

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})=J^{-1}\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X_{i}))}_{=:B}+o_{P}(1).

We then consider the decomposition of
B
following
[
Mackey et al. 2018
, eq.(11)]
:

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m\left(Z_{i},\theta_{0},h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)}_{=:B_{1}}

(63)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\cap S_{0}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}D^{\alpha}m\left(Z_{i},\theta_{0},h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{h}\left(X_{i}\right)-h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\alpha}}_{=:B_{2}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}D^{\alpha}m\left(Z_{i},\theta_{0},h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{h}\left(X_{i}\right)-h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\alpha}}_{=:B_{3}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\cap S_{1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}D^{\alpha}m_{1}\left(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\tilde{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{h}\left(X_{i}\right)-h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\alpha}}_{=:B_{4}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}D^{\alpha}m_{1}\left(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\tilde{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\left(\hat{h}\left(X_{i}\right)-h_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\alpha}}_{=:B_{5}}

where we recall that
{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}:\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k\}
.
First, it is easy to see that with high probability, it holds that

B_{1}\lesssim\mathrm{Var}\left(m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right)^{1/2}.

Second, by our assumption on the error
\hat{h}-h_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|B_{3}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0}}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|D^{\alpha}m\left(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}\left(X\right)\right)\left(\hat{h}\left(X\right)-h_{0}\left(X\right)\right)^{\alpha}\right|\right]

(64)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0}}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|D^{\alpha}m\left(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}\left(X\right)\right)\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\hat{h}\left(X\right)-h_{0}\left(X\right)\right|^{\alpha q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\star}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S_{0}}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\leq\sqrt{n}\lambda_{\star}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}\setminus S}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}},

where the last step follows from Holder’s inequality:

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|\hat{h}_{i}(X)-h_{0i}(X)\right|^{\alpha_{i}}\right\|_{P_{X},q}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\|\left|\hat{h}_{i}(X)-h_{0i}(X)\right|^{\alpha_{i}}\right\|_{P_{X},s_{i}/\alpha_{i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\|\hat{h}_{i}(X)-h_{0i}(X)\right\|_{P_{X},s_{i}}^{\alpha_{i}}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}.

Similarly, we have

\mathbb{E}[|B_{5}|]\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}\lambda_{\star}}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}.

Finally, the arguments in
[
Mackey et al. 2018
, Section A.2]
imply that
B_{2},B_{4}=o_{P}(1)
. Combining everything above, we conclude the proof of
Theorem

E.1
.

Under the assumptions in
Corollary

E.1
, it holds that
B_{3},B_{5}=o(n^{-1/2})
. As a result, the same arguments in
[
Mackey et al. 2018
, Section A.2]
would imply the desired asymptotic normality result in
Corollary

E.1
.

E.3
Proof of
Theorem

4.1
:
Theorem 4.1

(Structure-agnostic error from estimated moments)
.

The proof follows a similar argument as the proof of the previous theorem. Consider any probability distribution
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. We define
{\mathcal{E}}
as the ”good” event that the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
satisfies the conditions
Equation

12
,
Equation

13
,
Equation

14
and
Equation

15
. By assumption, we known that
\mathbb{P}[{\mathcal{E}}]\geq 1-\gamma/2
. Our subsequent analysis consider a
fixed
choice of
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
that fails into
{\mathcal{E}}
. Note that the moment function
\hat{m}_{r}(\cdot)
is partially linear in
q
, so for any index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
, if
\alpha_{2}\geq 2
, then
D^{\alpha}\hat{m}_{r}=0
. Now let’s calculate the derivative for
\alpha_{2}\in\{0,1\}
.

When
\alpha_{2}=0
, we have

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,h;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})

\displaystyle=\big[Y-q(X)-\theta(T-g(X))\big]\hat{J}_{r}^{(\alpha_{1})}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})+\theta\hat{J}_{r}^{(\alpha_{1}-1)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1}),

so that under
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,h;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid X\right]\right|=\left|\theta\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(\alpha_{1}-1)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\right]\right|\leq C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{1}-1}

for all
\alpha_{1}\leq j+1
. When
\alpha_{2}=1
, we have

D^{\alpha}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,q,g)=-\hat{J}_{r}^{(\alpha_{1})}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1}),

so that

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,q,g)\mid X\right]\right|=\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(\alpha_{1})}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid X\right]\right|\leq{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{1}}

for all
\alpha_{1}\leq j
. The above derivations also imply that

\displaystyle\|D^{(r+1,0)}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,h;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\|_{L^{s/2}(P)}

(65)

\displaystyle\leq\|Y-q(X)-\theta(T-g(X))\|_{L^{s}(P)}\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r+1)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}

\displaystyle+C_{\uptheta}\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\|Y-q(X)\|_{L^{s}(P)}+C_{\uptheta}\|T-g(X)\|_{L^{s}(P)}\big)\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r+1)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}

\displaystyle+C_{\uptheta}\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq 4(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}C_{\upeta})\sqrt{s}\big\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r+1)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}+\psi_{\uptheta}\big\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\big[4(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}C_{\upeta})\sqrt{s}+C_{\uptheta}\big]\Lambda_{r},

and

\|D^{(r,1)}\hat{m}_{r}(Z,\theta,h;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\|_{L^{s/2}(P)}\leq\big\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(r)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{s/2}(P)}\leq\Lambda_{r},

(66)

where we use the assumed property that

\max_{\beta\in\{r,r+1\}}\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{P,s}(h_{0},\Delta)}\big\|\hat{J}_{r}^{(\beta)}(T-g(X),X;{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\big\|_{L^{\infty}(P)}\leq\Lambda_{r}<+\infty.

Let
J({\mathcal{D}}_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{1}]
. Similar to the proof of the previous theorem, we consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}m(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X_{i});{\mathcal{D}}_{1})+o_{P}(n^{-1/2})

By Chebyshev’s inequality, with probability
\geq 1-\gamma/2
, we have

\Big|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}m(Z_{i},\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X_{i});{\mathcal{D}}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\big[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\big]\Big|\leq 2\gamma^{-1}V_{\mathsf{m}}n^{-1/2},

which implies that

|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}|\leq\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\big]+2\gamma^{-1}V_{\mathsf{m}}n^{-1/2}\big)+o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

Finally, by Taylor’s formula and the orthogonality condition,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\bigr]\bigr|

(67)

\displaystyle=\bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\bigr]\bigr|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{\alpha\in\{(j,0),(j-1,1)\}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\,\big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\,(\hat{h}(X)-h_{0}(X))^{\alpha}\bigr]\big|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\in\{(r+1,0),(r,1)\}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\,\big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},\tilde{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\,(\hat{h}(X)-h_{0}(X))^{\alpha}\bigr]\big|

\displaystyle(\text{where }\tilde{h}=h_{0}+t(\hat{h}-h_{0}),\ t\in[0,1])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{\alpha\in\{(j,0),(j-1,1)\}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\mid X\bigr](\hat{h}(X)-h_{0}(X))^{\alpha}\bigr|\Bigr]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\in\{(k+1,0),(k,1)\}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\,\bigl\|D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},\tilde{h}(X);{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\bigr\|_{L^{s/2}(P)}\,{\epsilon}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\,{\epsilon}_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{r}\Bigl(\frac{1}{j!}\,{\epsilon}^{(j)}{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}+\frac{1}{(j-1)!}\,{\epsilon}^{(j-1)}{\epsilon}_{1}^{j-1}{\epsilon}_{2}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\Bigl[\big(4(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\sqrt{s}+C_{\uptheta}\big){\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+r\,{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}\Bigr]\Lambda_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{1}{j!}\,\max\{{\epsilon}^{(j+1)},{\epsilon}^{(j)}\}\bigl({\epsilon}_{1}^{j+1}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{j}{\epsilon}_{2}\bigr)+\frac{1}{(r+1)!}\Bigl[\big(4(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\sqrt{s}+C_{\uptheta}\big){\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+r\,{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}\Bigr]\Lambda_{r},

where the third step uses Holder’s inequality and
s\geq 2r+2
.
This yields the desired bound. The total probability for this bound to hold is
(1-\gamma)^{2}\geq 1-2\gamma
, as desired.

E.4
Proof of
Lemma

4.1
:
Lemma 4.1

(Explicit formula for
J_{r}
)
.

We prove this lemma by induction on
r
. When
r=1
, the conclusion automatically holds by the assumed expression of
J_{1}(w,x)
. Now suppose that
r\geq 2
, and the conclusion holds for
r-1
, then by definition,

I_{r}(w,x)=\int_{0}^{w}J_{r-1}(w^{\prime},x)\textup{d}w^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{M_{r-1}}a_{ir}(x)\int_{0}^{w}\rho_{i,r-1}(w^{\prime})\textup{d}w=\sum_{i=1}^{M_{r-1}}a_{ir}(x)\rho_{i+1,r}(x)

and the conclusion follows.

Appendix F
Technical details in
Section

5.1

In this section, we provide additional results and details that complement
Section

5.1
.

We first consider the problem of estimating
\mu_{r}:=\mathbb{E}[\eta^{r}]
, where
r\geq 2
is some positive integer. It turns out that even under the independence noise assumption, there exists a fundamental bottleneck for estimating
\mu_{r}
, as stated in the following theorem. Interestingly, this statistical limit is different for
r=3
and all other values of
r
.

Theorem F.1

(Structure-agnostic limit for estimating residual moments)
.

Let
C_{\mathsf{T}}>0
be a constant and
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
be the set of all distributions of
(X,T)
generated from
T=g_{0}(X)+\eta
, such that
|T|\leq C_{\mathsf{T}},a.s.
and
\eta
is some mean-zero noise variable independent of
X
. Let
\Phi
be a mapping that maps
P_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
to the nuisance function
g_{0}
, and the target parameter is defined by
\theta(P_{0})=\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))^{r}]
. Then for any
\gamma\in(1/2,1)
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there exists a constant
c_{\gamma,r}>0
such that
\mathfrak{M}_{n,1-\gamma}\big({\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\epsilon}}(\hat{g})\big)\geq c_{\gamma}\left({\epsilon}^{\alpha_{r}}+n^{-1/2}\right)
, where
\alpha_{r}=3
if
r=3
and
\alpha_{r}=2
otherwise.
Moreover, these rates can be attained by
\theta=\theta_{r}
, where
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{+\infty}
is recursively defined as
\theta_{1}=0,\quad\theta_{k}=\mu_{k}^{\prime}-k\theta_{k-1}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\quad(k\geq 2),
where
\mu_{k}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i})\right)^{k}
.

The remaining part of this section is devoted to proving
Theorem

F.1
and
Theorem

5.2
.

F.1
Proof of
Theorem

F.1
:
Theorem F.1

(Structure-agnostic limit for estimating residual moments)
.

The proof is based on the method of fuzzy hypothesis, as introduced in
Lemma

A.1
. Let
X\sim P_{X}
be uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and
\eta
be a random variable independent of
X
, with density

p_{\eta}(z)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \exp(-z^{4})&\quad\text{if }z\leq 0\\
\exp(-1.5z^{4}+az^{3})&\quad\text{if }z>0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
a>0
is chosen such that
\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0
. Let
M
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{M})
where
\lambda_{i}
’s i.i.d. random variables such that
\lambda_{i}=2
with probability
\frac{1}{3}
and
=-1
with probability
\frac{2}{3}
. Let
B_{1},B_{2},\cdots,B_{M}
be a partition of
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1]
such that
P_{X}(B_{i})=\frac{1}{M},\forall i\in[M]
. Define
P_{\lambda}
to be the joint distribution of
(X,T)
generated from

X\sim P_{X},\quad T=g_{\lambda}(X)+\eta,

where
g_{\lambda}(x)=\hat{g}(x)+\frac{1}{2}{\epsilon}_{g}\Delta(\lambda,x)
, and
\hat{P}=\int P_{\lambda}\textup{d}\pi(\lambda)
. It is easy to see that
P_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
.

Let
\hat{p}
and
p_{\lambda}
be the density of
\hat{P}
and
\hat{P}_{\lambda}
respectively, then the above definitions and Taylor’s formula together imply that

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}(x,t)

\displaystyle=p_{X}(x)p_{\eta}(t-g_{\lambda}(x))=p_{\eta}(t-\hat{g}(x)-{\epsilon}_{g}\Delta(\lambda,x)/2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{(-2)^{i}i!}\Delta(\lambda,x)^{i}{\epsilon}_{g}^{i}p_{\eta}^{(i)}(t-\hat{g}(x)).

For any given
X=x
,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\Delta(\lambda,x)^{i}]=\frac{2}{3}(2^{i-1}+(-1)^{i})
is independent of
x
, thus
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[p_{\lambda}(x,t)]
only depends on
x,t
through
t-\hat{g}(x)
. As a result, we can define a random variable
\hat{\eta}
which is independent of
X
and has density
p_{\hat{\eta}}(t-\hat{g}(x))=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[p_{\lambda}(x,t)]
. The data generating process

X\sim P_{X},\quad T=\hat{g}(X)+\hat{\eta}

thus induces a density
p_{X}(x)p_{\hat{\eta}}(t-\hat{g}(x))=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[p_{\lambda}(x,t)]=\hat{p}(x,t)
.

We choose
P=\hat{P}
,
Q_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}=B_{j}\times{\mathcal{T}}
in
Lemma

A.2
, the corresponding
p_{j}=\frac{1}{M}
. For any
t\in{\mathcal{T}},x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
\lambda\in\mathrm{supp}(\pi)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\eta}(t-\hat{g}(x)-{\epsilon}_{g}\Delta(\lambda,x)/2)^{2}}{p_{\hat{\eta}}(t-\hat{g}(x))}\leq\frac{p_{\eta}(t-\hat{g}(x)-{\epsilon}_{g})^{2}}{p_{\eta}(t-\hat{g}(x)+{\epsilon}_{g}/2)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-2(t-\hat{g}(x)-{\epsilon}_{g})^{4}+1.5(t-\hat{g}(x)+{\epsilon}_{g}/2)^{4}+a|t-\hat{g}(x)+{\epsilon}_{g}/2|^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\exp\left(-(t-\hat{g}(x))^{4}/2+11|t-\hat{g}(x)|^{3}{\epsilon}_{g}+9|t-\hat{g}(x)|{\epsilon}_{g}^{3}/2+a|t-\hat{g}(x)+{\epsilon}_{g}/2|^{3}\right)}_{\Gamma(t-\hat{g}(x))}.

since
\hat{g}
is assumed to be uniformly bounded, it is easy to see that
x\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(t-\hat{g}(x))\textup{d}t
is uniformly bounded as well.
Therefore, in the setting of
Lemma

A.2
, we have

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=M\max_{j}\sup_{\lambda}\int_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}}\frac{\left(p_{\lambda}-\hat{p}\right)^{2}}{p}\textup{d}\mu

\displaystyle\leq M\max_{j}\sup_{\lambda}\int_{B_{j}}\textup{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\hat{p}(x,t)+\frac{p_{\lambda}(x,t)^{2}}{\hat{p}(x,t)}\right)\textup{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\sup_{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\hat{p}(x,t)+\frac{p_{\lambda}(x,t)^{2}}{\hat{p}(x,t)}\right)\textup{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\sup_{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\hat{p}(x,t)+\Gamma(t-\hat{g}(x))\right)\textup{d}t

\displaystyle<+\infty

is bounded by some universal constant, which we denote by
\bar{b}
. Let
C
be the constant in
Lemma

A.2
that corresponds to
A=1
, and choose
M\geq\max\left\{n\max\{1,\bar{b}\},C\delta^{-1}n^{2}\bar{b}^{2}\right\}
, then we have that

H\left(\hat{P}^{\otimes n},\int P_{\lambda}^{\otimes n}d\pi(\lambda)\right)\leq\delta.

The final step is to verify the separation condition (
27
). Specifically, we choose
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
and define
T(P)
to be
-\mu_{r}
for any
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. We abuse notation and use
\mu_{r}(P)
to denote the value of
\mu_{r}
corresponds to
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{r}(\hat{P})

\displaystyle=\int z^{r}p_{\hat{\eta}}(z)\textup{d}z=\mu_{r}(P_{\lambda})+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{2^{i-1}+(-1)^{i}}{3(-2)^{i-1}i!}{\epsilon}_{g}^{i}\int z^{r}p_{\eta}^{(i)}(z)\textup{d}z+{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon}_{g}^{4}).

Note that for
i\leq r
we have

\int z^{r}p_{\eta}^{(i)}(z)\textup{d}z=(-1)^{i}\frac{r!}{(r-i)!}\int z^{r-i}p_{\eta}(z)\textup{d}z.

In particular, we consider the case where
i=2
. If
r\neq 3
then the above equation is nonzero, implying that

\mu_{r}(\hat{P})=\mu_{r}(P_{\lambda})+\Theta({\epsilon}_{g}^{2}).

Therefore,
Lemma

A.1
can be applied with
s=\Theta({\epsilon}_{g}^{2})
, which yields the desired result.

If
r=3
, then
\int z^{r-2}p_{\eta}(z)\textup{d}z=\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0
, so that

\mu_{r}(\hat{P})=\mu_{r}(P_{\lambda})+\Theta({\epsilon}_{g}^{3}),

and the conclusion can be similarly derived.

F.2
Proofs of
Theorem

5.1
and
Theorem

5.2

In this subsection, we present the proofs of
Theorem

5.1
and
Theorem

5.2
. The proof techniques are largely similar. We choose to start with the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, which is more involved.
3
3

3

Although
Theorem

5.1
consider a more general class of noise, the rate is strictly looser in the setting of
Theorem

5.2
, as discussed in
Remark

5.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2

For any
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, let
\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{k}]
, then it is easy to see that

|\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq 2^{k-1}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[(T-g_{0}(X))^{k}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[(g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X))^{k}\big]\big)\leq 2^{2k}\big(k^{k/2}\psi_{\upeta}^{k}+C_{\mathsf{g}}^{k}\big).

(68)

By Chebyshev’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|>\delta_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\mathrm{Var}\left((T-\hat{g}(X))^{k}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\mathbb{E}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2k}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(\eta+g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X)\big)^{2k}\right]=\frac{2^{4k}(C_{\mathsf{g}}^{2k}+k^{k}\psi_{\upeta}^{2k})}{\delta_{k}^{2}n},

where the last step uses
|g|,|\hat{g}|\leq C_{\mathsf{g}}
and
\|\eta\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\psi_{\upeta}
.
We choose
\delta_{k}=r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}2^{2k}(C_{\mathsf{g}}^{2k}+k^{k}\psi_{\upeta}^{2k})^{1/2}
, then it is easy to see that with probability
\geq 1-\gamma
,
|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{k}
for all
k\in[r]
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all these inequalities hold. The following lemma bounds the difference between
\theta
and its population version (with
\mu_{l}^{\prime}
replaced by
\bar{\mu}_{l}^{\prime}
), defined as

\bar{\theta}_{r}=(-1)^{r+1}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\bar{\mu}_{2}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\bar{\mu}_{3}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{2}^{\prime}&\binom{2}{1}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\bar{\mu}_{r}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{r-1}^{\prime}&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\binom{r-1}{r-2}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}\end{array}\right|,

Lemma F.1

(Moment–to–cumulant type bounds)
.

Let the sequences
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
,
\{\bar{\theta}_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
,

\{\mu_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k\geq 1}
satisfy the recursions

\displaystyle\theta_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k}^{\prime},\quad\bar{\theta}_{1}=\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}

(69)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\mu_{k-j}^{\prime}\theta_{j},

(70)

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}\bar{\theta}_{j}.

(71)

Assume there exist constants
C_{\mathsf{g}},\psi_{\upeta}>0
and

l,\gamma,n\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
k\geq 1

\displaystyle|\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2k}\!\Bigl(C_{\mathsf{g}}^{\,k}+k^{k/2}\psi_{\upeta}^{\,k}\Bigr),

(72)

\displaystyle|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\,2^{2k}\!\Bigl(C_{\mathsf{g}}^{\,2k}+k^{k}\psi_{\upeta}^{\,2k}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

(73)

Then, for all
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|\bar{\theta}_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq\big[8k(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{k},

(74)

\displaystyle|\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 3\big[12k(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{k}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}.

(75)

Proof
:

Throughout the argument write

A_{k}:=2^{2k}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{k}k^{k/2},\qquad D_{k}:=r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\,A_{k}.

Equation

72
–
Equation

73
imply that
|\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq A_{k}

and
|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq D_{k}
. By Triangle inequality,
Equation

70
implies that

|\theta_{k}|\leq A_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{j-1}A_{k-j}|\theta_{j}|.

Let
\bar{\theta}_{k}=A_{k}\rho_{k}
, then this becomes

|\rho_{k}|\leq 1+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{j-1}\frac{A_{j}A_{k-j}}{A_{k}}|\rho_{j}|.

(76)

We now prove that

\binom{k-1}{j-1}\frac{A_{j}A_{k-j}}{A_{k}}\leq\frac{k^{k/2}}{j^{j/2}(k-j)^{(k-j)/2}}.

(77)

Indeed, since

\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}<n!<\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}e^{\frac{1}{12n}},\quad\forall n\geq 1,

we can deduce that

\binom{k-1}{j-1}\leq\binom{k}{j}<\frac{\sqrt{2\pi k}(k/e)^{k}\exp(1/(12k))}{\sqrt{2\pi j}(j/e)^{j}\cdot\sqrt{2\pi(k-j)}((k-j)/e)^{k-j}}\leq\frac{k^{k}}{j^{j}(k-j)^{k-j}}.

Hence,

\binom{k-1}{j-1}\frac{A_{j}A_{k-j}}{A_{k}}\leq\frac{k^{k}}{j^{j}(k-j)^{k-j}}\cdot\frac{j^{j/2}(k-j)^{(k-j)/2}}{k^{k/2}}\leq\frac{k^{k/2}}{j^{j/2}(k-j)^{(k-j)/2}},

proving
Equation

77
. Plugging into
Equation

76
and rearranging, we obtain

k^{-k/2}|\rho_{k}|\leq k^{-k/2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)^{-(k-j)/2}j^{-j/2}|\rho_{j}|\leq k^{-k/2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j^{-j/2}|\rho_{j}|.

|\rho_{k}|\leq 3^{k-1}(k-1)!,\quad\forall k\geq 1.

(78)

Define

S_{k}\;:=\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}i^{-i/2}|\rho_{i}|,\quad k\geq 1.

Then we have that

\displaystyle S_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\;2S_{k-1}+k^{-k/2}

(79)

\displaystyle S_{k}+k^{-k/2}

\displaystyle\leq\;2\big(S_{k-1}+(k-1)^{-(k-1)/2}\big).

Moreover,

S_{1}=|\rho_{1}|=\frac{|\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}|}{4(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})}\leq\frac{{\epsilon}_{1}}{4\psi_{\upeta}}\leq\frac{1}{4},

so it follows from
Equation

79
that
S_{k}\leq 2^{k}
. Finally, we have

|\bar{\theta}_{k}|\leq A_{k}|\rho_{k}|\leq A_{k}\cdot 2^{k}k^{k/2}\leq\big[8k(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{k}.

We now turn to bound
\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}
.
Set
\Delta_{k}:=\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}
.
Subtracting (
70
) from (
71
) gives

\Delta_{k}=(\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}-\mu_{k}^{\prime})-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\!\Bigl[(\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}-\mu_{k-j}^{\prime})\bar{\theta}_{j}+\mu_{k-j}^{\prime}\Delta_{j}\Bigr].

Taking absolute values and invoking the bounds already proved yields

\displaystyle|\Delta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\Bigl(D_{k-j}\,|\bar{\theta}_{j}|+(|\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}|+D_{k-j})\,|\Delta_{j}|\Bigr)

(80)

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\Bigl[r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}A_{k-j}\cdot 2^{j}j^{j/2}A_{j}+(|\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}|+D_{k-j})\,|\Delta_{j}|\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}k^{k/2}A_{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)^{-(k-j)/2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}(A_{k-j}+D_{k-j})|\Delta_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+4r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}k^{k/2}A_{k}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}A_{k-j}|\Delta_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq 8r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}k^{k/2}A_{k}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}A_{k-j}|\Delta_{j}|.

Let
\Delta_{k}=A_{k}\delta_{k}
, then
Equation

77
and
Equation

80
implies that

\displaystyle|\delta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 8r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}k^{k/2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\frac{A_{k-j}A_{j}}{A_{k}}|\delta_{j}|

(81)

\displaystyle\leq 8r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}k^{k/2}+2k^{k/2}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)^{-(k-j)/2}j^{-j/2}|\delta_{j}|

Define

T_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}i^{-i/2}|\delta_{i}|,\quad k\geq 1.

Then we have that

\displaystyle T_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\;T_{k-1}+8r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(k-j)^{-(k-j)/2}j^{-j/2}|\delta_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq\;8r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}+3T_{k-1}

which further implies

T_{k}+4r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\leq 3\big(T_{k-1}+4r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\big).

(82)

Moreover,

T_{1}=|\delta_{1}|=\frac{|\mu_{k}-\mu_{k}^{\prime}|}{4(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})}\leq\frac{1}{4}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2},

so it follows from
Equation

82
that
T_{r}\leq 3^{r+1}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}
. Therefore,
|\Delta_{r}|=A_{r}|\delta_{r}|\leq r^{r/2}T_{r}A_{r}\leq r^{r/2}3^{r+1}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}A_{r}
, concluding the proof.

\square

In view of the previous lemma, we only need to bound the difference between
\bar{\theta}_{l}
and
\kappa_{l}
. Note the following well-known property of cumulants
\{\kappa_{l}\}_{l=1}^{+\infty}
:

\log\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[e^{t\eta}]=\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}\kappa_{l}\frac{t^{l}}{l!}.

Let
\hat{\eta}=T-\hat{g}(X)
and
D_{X}=g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X)
, then
\hat{\eta}=\eta+D_{X}
and
\eta,D_{X}
are independent by our assumption. By definition,
\bar{\theta}_{l}
is the
l
-th order cumulant of
T-\hat{g}(X)
. Hence,

\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}\bar{\theta}_{l}\frac{t^{l}}{l!}=\log\mathbb{E}[e^{t\hat{\eta}}]=\log\mathbb{E}[e^{t\eta}]+\log\mathbb{E}[e^{tD_{X}}]=\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}(\kappa_{l}+d_{l})\frac{t^{l}}{l!},

where
d_{l}
is the
l
-th order cumulant of the variable
D_{X}
. From
Proposition

A.2
we can deduce that
d_{r}\leq(2r{\epsilon})^{r}
. combining with
Lemma

F.1
, the conclusion follows.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1

For any
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, let
\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{P}[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{k}]
, then it is easy to see that

|\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq 2^{k-1}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[|T|^{k}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{g}(X)|^{k}\big]\big)\leq 2^{k}C_{\mathsf{T}}^{k}.

(83)

By Chebyshev’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|>\delta_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\mathrm{Var}\left((T-\hat{g}(X))^{k}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}n}\mathbb{E}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2k}\right]\leq\frac{2^{2k}C_{\mathsf{T}}^{2k}}{\delta_{k}^{2}n}.

We choose
\delta_{k}=r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}
, then it is easy to see that with probability
\geq 1-\gamma
,
|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{k}
for all
k\in[l]
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all these inequalities hold. The following lemma bounds the difference between
\theta
and its population version (with
\mu_{l}^{\prime}
replaced by
\bar{\mu}_{l}^{\prime}
), defined as

\bar{\theta}_{r}=(-1)^{r+1}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\bar{\mu}_{2}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\bar{\mu}_{3}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{2}^{\prime}&\binom{2}{1}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}&1&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\bar{\mu}_{r}^{\prime}&\bar{\mu}_{r-1}^{\prime}&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\binom{r-1}{r-2}\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}\end{array}\right|,

Lemma F.2

(Moment–to–cumulant type bounds)
.

Let the sequences
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
,
\{\bar{\theta}_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
,

\{\mu_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k\geq 1}
satisfy the recursions

\displaystyle\theta_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k}^{\prime},\quad\bar{\theta}_{1}=\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}

(84)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\mu_{k-j}^{\prime}\theta_{j},

(85)

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{k}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}\bar{\theta}_{j}.

(86)

Assume there exist constants
C_{\mathsf{g}},\psi_{\upeta}>0
and

l,\gamma,n\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
k\geq 1

\displaystyle|\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k},

(87)

\displaystyle|\mu_{k}^{\prime}-\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}.

(88)

Then, for all
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|\bar{\theta}_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 2(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}(k-1)!,

(89)

\displaystyle|\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 10k^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(2C_{\mathrm{T}})^{k}(k-1)!.

(90)

Proof
:

By Triangle inequality,
Equation

70
implies that

|\theta_{k}|\leq(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{j-1}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k-j}|\theta_{j}|.

Let
\bar{\theta}_{k}=(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}\rho_{k}
, then this becomes

|\rho_{k}|\leq 1+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{j-1}|\rho_{j}|.

(91)

Since

|\rho_{1}|=\frac{|\bar{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}|}{2C_{\mathsf{T}}}\leq\frac{{\epsilon}_{1}}{2C_{\mathsf{T}}}\leq\frac{1}{2},

it is easy to prove by induction that

|\rho_{k}|\leq 2(k-1)!,

so that

|\bar{\theta}_{k}|\leq 2(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}(k-1)!.

We now turn to bound
\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}
.
Set
\Delta_{k}:=\bar{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}
.
Subtracting (
70
) from (
71
) gives

\Delta_{k}=(\bar{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}-\mu_{k}^{\prime})-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\!\Bigl[(\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}-\mu_{k-j}^{\prime})\bar{\theta}_{j}+\mu_{k-j}^{\prime}\Delta_{j}\Bigr].

Let
D_{k}=r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(2C_{\mathrm{T}})^{k}
.
Taking absolute values and invoking the bounds already proved yields

\displaystyle|\Delta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\Bigl(D_{k-j}|\bar{\theta}_{j}|+(|\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}|+D_{k-j})\,|\Delta_{j}|\Bigr)

(92)

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}\Bigl[D_{k-j}\cdot 2(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{j}(j-1)!+(|\bar{\mu}_{k-j}^{\prime}|+D_{k-j})\,|\Delta_{j}|\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq D_{k}+2D_{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{(k-j)!}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}(A_{k-j}+D_{k-j})|\Delta_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq 5D_{k}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}A_{k-j}|\Delta_{j}|.

Let
\Delta_{k}=D_{k}\delta_{k}
, then
Equation

77
and
Equation

80
implies that

\displaystyle|\delta_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq 5+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{\,j-1\,}|\delta_{j}|.

(93)

We also have that

|\delta_{1}|=\frac{|\mu_{k}-\mu_{k}^{\prime}|}{4(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})}\leq\frac{1}{4}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2},

so by induction we can deduce that
|\delta_{k}|\leq 10(k-1)!
, and

|\Delta_{k}|\leq 10r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{k}(k-1)!,

concluding the proof.

\square

In view of the previous lemma, we only need to bound the difference between
\bar{\theta}_{l}
and
\kappa_{l}
. Note the following well-known property of cumulants
\{\kappa_{l}\}_{l=1}^{+\infty}
:

\log\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[e^{t\eta}]=\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}\kappa_{l}\frac{t^{l}}{l!}.

Let
\hat{\eta}=T-\hat{g}(X)
and
D_{X}=g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X)
, then
\hat{\eta}=\eta+D_{X}
and
\eta,D_{X}
are independent by our assumption. By definition,
\bar{\theta}_{l}
is the
l
-th order cumulant of
T-\hat{g}(X)
. Hence,

\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}\bar{\theta}_{l}\frac{t^{l}}{l!}=\log\mathbb{E}[e^{t\hat{\eta}}]=\log\mathbb{E}[e^{t\eta}]+\log\mathbb{E}[e^{tD_{X}}]=\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}(\kappa_{l}+d_{l})\frac{t^{l}}{l!},

where
d_{l}
is the
l
-th order cumulant of the variable
D_{X}
, and we have that
d_{l}\leq l!\mathbb{E}\left[\left|g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X)\right|^{l}\right]\leq l!{\epsilon}^{l}
, and the conclusion follows.

F.3
Relaxing the independent noise assumption

In this subsection, we consider a case where the noise
\eta
is almost independent of
X
and show that our estimator is still better than the plug-in estimator.

Proposition F.1
