(Finite–sample accuracy of two cubic–moment estimators)
.

Let
T=g_{0}(X)+\eta
with
\eta={\epsilon}_{0}\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
and assume

1.

\mathbb{E}[\eta_{i}]=0,\ \mathrm{Var}(\eta_{i})=\sigma_{i}^{2}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
;

2.

\eta_{1}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X
(while making no restriction on the joint law of
\eta_{0}
and
X
);

3.

\eta_{0},\eta_{1}
have finite third moments;

4.

an estimator
\hat{g}
satisfies
\delta(X):=\hat{g}_{0}(X)-g_{0}(X)
with
\|\delta\|_{L^{3}(P_{X})}\leq{\epsilon}
;

5.

\mathbb{E}[\,|T-\hat{g}_{0}(X)|^{6}\,]<\infty
and set
M:=\mathbb{E}[\,|T-\hat{g}_{0}(X)|^{6}\,]^{1/6}
.

Given i.i.d. samples
\{(X_{i},T_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, put
Z_{i}:=T_{i}-\hat{g}_{0}(X_{i})
and define

\hat{\mu}_{k,n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{k}\quad(k=1,2,3),\qquad\hat{\psi}_{n}:=\hat{\mu}_{3,n}-3\hat{\mu}_{2,n}\hat{\mu}_{1,n},\qquad\hat{\nu}_{n}:=\hat{\mu}_{3,n}.

For any
0<\delta<1
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle|\hat{\psi}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}]|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{6\sigma_{0}^{2}{\epsilon}_{0}^{2}{\epsilon}+3\sigma_{0}{\epsilon}_{0}{\epsilon}^{2}+4{\epsilon}^{3}}_{\text{bias}}+C\,M^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{n}},

(94)

\displaystyle|\hat{\nu}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}]|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{3\sigma_{1}^{2}{\epsilon}+3\sigma_{0}^{2}{\epsilon}_{0}^{2}{\epsilon}+3\sigma_{0}{\epsilon}_{0}{\epsilon}^{2}+{\epsilon}^{3}}_{\text{bias}}+C\,M^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{n}},

(95)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant (e.g.
C=10
). Consequently, if
{\epsilon}_{0}\to 0
and
{\epsilon}\to 0
while
n\to\infty
, the plug–in estimator
\hat{\psi}_{n}
is
o({\epsilon})
–biased and
\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-1/2})
–consistent, whereas the naive estimator
\hat{\nu}_{n}
keeps a leading
\Theta({\epsilon})
bias whenever
\sigma_{1}^{2}>0
.

Proof
:

With
Z=T-\hat{g}_{0}(X)=\eta-\delta
and
\mu_{k}:=\mathbb{E}[Z^{k}]
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}]

\displaystyle=(\mu_{3}-3\mu_{2}\mu_{1}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}])+\bigl[(\hat{\mu}_{3,n}-\mu_{3})-3\mu_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{1,n}-\mu_{1})-3(\hat{\mu}_{2,n}-\mu_{2})\hat{\mu}_{1,n}\bigr],

(96)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{n}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}]

\displaystyle=(\mu_{3}-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}])+(\hat{\mu}_{3,n}-\mu_{3}).

(97)

The first bracket in each line is the
bias
, the second the
sampling error
.

Now write
\eta={\epsilon}_{0}\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
,
\delta=\delta(X)
. Expanding moments and using
\eta_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp X
,

\mu_{1}=-\mathbb{E}[\delta],\quad\mu_{2}=\sigma_{1}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{0}^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\delta^{2}],\quad\mu_{3}=\mathbb{E}[\eta^{3}]-3\sigma_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\delta]-3{\epsilon}_{0}^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\delta]-\mathbb{E}[\delta^{3}]+3{\epsilon}_{0}\mathbb{E}[\eta_{0}\delta^{2}].

Hölder yields
|\mathbb{E}[\delta]|\leq{\epsilon}
,
\mathbb{E}[\delta^{2}]\leq{\epsilon}^{2}
,
|\mathbb{E}[\delta^{3}]|\leq{\epsilon}^{3}
, while Cauchy–Schwarz gives
|\mathbb{E}[\eta_{0}\delta^{2}]|\leq\sigma_{0}{\epsilon}^{2}\!.

Substituting into (
96
)–(
97
) gives the bias terms displayed in (
94
)–(
95
).

Define
\Delta_{k}:=\hat{\mu}_{k,n}-\mu_{k}
. By Bernstein’s inequality for centred variables with sixth moment
M^{6}
,

\Pr\Bigl(|\Delta_{k}|>t\Bigr)\leq 2\exp\!\Bigl(-\tfrac{nt^{2}}{2\mathbb{E}[Z^{2k}]+2Mt/3}\Bigr)\quad(k=1,2,3).

Taking
t_{k}:=M^{k}\sqrt{(2\log(6/\delta))/n}
and a union bound over
k
ensures
|\Delta_{k}|\leq t_{k}
with probability
1-\delta
. Using
|\mu_{1}|\leq M
,
|\mu_{2}|\leq M^{2}
and (
96
),

|\hat{\psi}_{n}-\mu_{3}+3\mu_{2}\mu_{1}|\leq|\Delta_{3}|+3M^{2}|\Delta_{1}|+3M|\Delta_{2}|+3|\Delta_{2}||\Delta_{1}|\leq 10M^{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\delta)}{n}}.

An analogous bound holds for
|\hat{\nu}_{n}-\mu_{3}|
. Combining with the bias bounds completes (
94
)–(
95
).

\square

Appendix G
Technical details in
Section

5.2

In this section, we provide detailed proofs of results in
Section

5.2
. We let
\mathrm{P}_{m}
be the set of all possible partitions of the integer
m
, i.e., the set of all multisets of positive integers that sum to
m
(
e.g.
, for
m=4
the possible partitions are
(4),(1,3),(2,2),(1,1,2),(1,1,1,1)
), and
\mathrm{P}_{m,j}
be the set of partitions with
j
terms. We define
p(m)=|\mathrm{P}_{m}|
and
p(m,j)=|\mathrm{P}_{m,j}|
respectively. Note that
\mathrm{P}_{m}
is different from
\Pi_{m}
defined in
Section

5
, which is the number of partitions of
[m]
into distinct subsets.

Proposition G.1

(Partition number bound)
.

We have
p(m)\leq 2^{m}
for all
m\geq 1
.

Proof
:

Consider placing numbers
1,2,\cdots,m
in a row and delimiters are placed between some consecutive numbers. Clearly, the total number of ways to place the delimiters is
2^{m-1}
. For each partition
m=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{j}
, there exists at least one way of placing the delimiters, such that their induced partition of
\{1,2,\cdots,m\}
contains subsets of sizes
i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}
. This creates an injective mapping from
\mathrm{P}_{m}
to the set of possible delimiters, implying that
p(m)\leq 2^{m-1}
.

\square

G.1
Proof of
Theorem

5.4
:
Theorem 5.4

(ACE estimation error: sub-Gaussian noise)
.

Lemma G.1

(Log inequalities)
.

Let
a,b>0
.

1.

For every

0<x\leq\dfrac{b}{a}-\dfrac{1}{a}\log\dfrac{b}{a}

we have

ax+\log x\;\leq\;b.

2.

Assume, in addition, that
ab\geq e
(so
\log(ab)\geq 1
).
Then for every

0<x\leq\frac{b}{a\log(ab)}

we have

x\log(ax)\;\leq\;b.

Proof
:

Proof of
Item

1
.

Define

g(x)\triangleq ax+\log x-b,\;x>0.

Because
g^{\prime}(x)=a+1/x>0
, the map
g
is strictly increasing.
Put

x_{0}\triangleq\frac{b}{a}-\frac{1}{a}\log\frac{b}{a}\quad(>0).

We show
g(x_{0})\leq 0
.
Set
y\triangleq\dfrac{b}{a}
; then

g(x_{0})=a\Bigl(y-\tfrac{1}{a}\log y\Bigr)+\log\Bigl(y-\tfrac{1}{a}\log y\Bigr)-b=-\,\log y+\log\!\Bigl(y-\tfrac{1}{a}\log y\Bigr).

Since
y-\tfrac{1}{a}\log y<y
, the argument of the second logarithm is
smaller than
y
, hence

\log\bigl(y-\tfrac{1}{a}\log y\bigr)<\log y
and
g(x_{0})<0
.
Because
g
is increasing,
x\leq x_{0}
implies
g(x)\leq g(x_{0})\leq 0
,
i.e.
ax+\log x\leq b
.

Proof of
Item

2
.

Define

h(x)\triangleq x\log(ax)-b,\;x>0.

We have
h^{\prime}(x)=\log(ax)+1
, so
h
is strictly increasing for

x\geq e^{-1}/a
. The equation
h(x)=0
has the unique positive root

x^{\star}=\frac{b}{a\,W(ab)},

where
W
is the Lambert-
W
function
(the solution of
z=W(z)e^{W(z)}
). When
ab\geq e
we have

W(ab)\geq\log(ab)
(standard lower bound for
W
on
[e,\infty)
),
hence

x^{\star}=\frac{b}{a\,W(ab)}\;\geq\;\frac{b}{a\log(ab)}.

Thus every
x\leq b/(a\log(ab))
satisfies
x\leq x^{\star}
and,
by monotonicity of
h
,
h(x)\leq h(x^{\star})=0
; that is

x\log(ax)\leq b
.

\square

Lemma G.2

(Condition for bias domination)
.

Let

\Delta_{i}\triangleq\bigl|\kappa_{i}-\hat{\kappa}_{i}\bigr|\;\leq\;\underbrace{3\big[12i(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{i}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}}_{\text{variance}}+\underbrace{(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{\,i}}_{\text{bias}},\qquad 1\leq i\leq r.

Let
a\triangleq 2\log\big(6(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}){\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}\big),b\triangleq\log(\gamma n/9)
with
a,b>0
.
If

l\;\leq\;\frac{b}{\,a\,}-\frac{1}{\,a\,}\log\!\frac{b}{\,a\,},

(98)

then for every
1\leq i\leq r

3\big[12i(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{i}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\;\leq\;(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{\,i},

i.e. the bias term
(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{\,i}
dominates the variance term in

Lemma

G.2
.

Proof
:

Note that

\frac{\text{variance}}{\text{bias}}=3\big[6(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}){\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}\big]^{i}r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\leq 1,\quad\forall i\in[r]

is equivalent to

2\log\big(6(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}){\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}\big)l+\log l\leq\log(\gamma n/9).

We can apply
Lemma

G.1
with
a=2\log\big(6(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}){\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}\big)
and
b=\log(\gamma n/9)
to obtain the desired conclusion.

\square

Let

\displaystyle J_{r}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}a_{ir}w^{i-1},

(99)

\displaystyle a_{1k}

\displaystyle=1,a_{ir}=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|},2\leq i\leq r+1.

be the exact version of
\hat{J}_{r}
. We first derive some bounds for the coefficients of
J_{r}
and
\hat{J}_{r}
.

Lemma G.3

(Bounding polynomial coefficients)
.

For any
i\in[r+1]
we have

|a_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}.

(100)

Proof
:

By definition, we have

\displaystyle|a_{ir}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}

(101)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\prod_{B\in\pi}2^{2|B|}|B|^{|B|/2}\psi_{\upeta}^{|B|}

(102)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}(4\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}|B|^{|B|/2}

(103)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}(4\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j})\in\mathrm{P}_{r+1-i,j}}\binom{r+1-i}{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}i_{s}^{i_{s}/2}

(104)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(4\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}p(r+1-i,j)

(105)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i},

(106)

where
Equation

101
follows from triangle inequality,
Equation

102
follows from the cumulant bound in
Proposition

A.2
,
Equation

104
rearranges the summation term according to the number of subsets in the partition
\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}
,
Equation

105
follows from

\binom{r+1-i}{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}i_{s}^{i_{s}}=(r+1-i)!\prod_{s=1}^{j}\frac{i_{s}^{i_{s}/2}}{i_{s}!}\leq(r+1-i)!,

and
Equation

106
follows from
Proposition

G.1
.

\square

Lemma G.4

(Bounding the estimation error of polynomial coefficients)
.

For any
i\in[r+1]
we have

|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(16\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

(107)

Proof
:

By definition, we have

\displaystyle|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\bigg|\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\bigg(\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}-\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\bigg|\bigg(\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}-\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\sum_{B\in\pi}|\kappa_{|B|}-\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}|\cdot\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi\setminus\{B\}}\max\big\{\big|\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}\big|,\big|\hat{\kappa}_{|B^{\prime}|}\big|\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\sum_{B\in\pi}\big(2|B|{\epsilon}_{1}\big)^{|B|}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi\setminus\{B\}}2^{2|B^{\prime}|+1}|B^{\prime}|^{|B^{\prime}|/2}\psi_{\upeta}^{\,|B^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j})\in\mathrm{P}_{r+1-i,j}}\binom{r+1-i}{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}i_{s}^{i_{s}/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}p(r+1-i,j)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(16\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1},

as desired. Here in the last but one step, we use the fact that

\binom{r+1-i}{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}i_{s}^{i_{s}}=(r+1-i)!\prod_{s=1}^{j}\frac{i_{s}^{i_{s}/2}}{i_{s}!}\leq(r+1-i)!,

and the last step follows from
Proposition

G.1
.

\square

Lemma G.5

(Key lemma; approximate orthogonality)
.

Let

r\;\leq\;\frac{b}{\,a\,}-\frac{1}{\,a\,}\log\!\frac{b}{\,a\,},

(108)

where
a\triangleq 2\log\big(6(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}){\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}\big),b\triangleq\log(\gamma n/9)
as in
Lemma

G.2
, then under
{\mathcal{E}}
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\leq(4e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r-k}.

Proof
:

For any
k\in[r]
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=k}^{r}\frac{i!}{(i-k)!}\hat{a}_{i+1,r}\mu_{i-k}

(109)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=k}^{r}\frac{1}{(r-i)!(i-k)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\cdot\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{i-k}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(r-k)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r-k}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\left(\kappa_{|B|}-\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\right).

By
Lemma

G.2
, we have
|\kappa_{i}-\hat{\kappa}_{i}|\leq 2(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{i}
for all
i\in[r]
.
Then for any
1\leq j\leq r-k
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r-k},|\pi|=j}\left|\prod_{B\in\pi}\left(\kappa_{|B|}-\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j})\in\mathrm{P}_{r+1-k,j}}\binom{r-k}{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}2(i_{s}{\epsilon}_{1})^{i_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r-k}(r-k)!{\epsilon}_{1}^{r-k}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j})\in\mathrm{P}_{r-k,j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}\frac{i_{s}^{i_{s}}}{i_{s}!}

\displaystyle\leq(r-k)!p(r-k,j)(2e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r-k}.

Plugging into
Equation

109
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]

\displaystyle\leq p(l-k)(2e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r-k}\leq(4e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r-k}.

\square

Lemma G.6

(Identifiability coefficient)
.

\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))]=\frac{1}{r!}\kappa_{r+1}
.

Proof
:

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))]

(110)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\mu_{i}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{i}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}.

Consider any partition
\pi\in\Pi_{i}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
1\in B_{1}
and
|B_{1}|=k,k\leq i
. There are a total of
\binom{i-1}{k-1}
possible choices of
B_{1}
, and the remaining sets form a partition of
[i-k]
. Hence we can write

\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{i}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\binom{i-1}{k-1}\sum_{\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\Pi_{i-r}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}\in\pi^{\prime\prime}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime\prime}|}.

Plugging into
Equation

110
, the right-hand side

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!}\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\binom{r}{k-1}\kappa_{k}\sum_{i=r}^{r+1}\binom{r-k+1}{i-r}\sum_{\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\Pi_{i-r}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}\in\pi^{\prime\prime}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime\prime}|}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!}\bigg[\kappa_{r+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\binom{r}{k-1}\kappa_{k}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-k}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\big(\kappa_{|B|}-\kappa_{|B|}\big)\bigg]=\frac{1}{r!}\kappa_{r+1},

concluding the proof.

\square

Lemma G.7

(Linear–in–
\varepsilon_{1}
moment difference)
.

Let

T=g_{0}(X)+\eta,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0,\qquad\eta\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X,\qquad\|\eta\|_{\psi_{2}}=:\psi_{\eta}<\infty.

Assume an estimate
\hat{g}
satisfies

\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{L^{s}(P)}\leq{\epsilon}_{1}\leq\psi_{\upeta},\quad\text{for some }s\geq 2,

and fix an integer
1\leq i\leq s/2
.
Then

\mathbb{E}\bigl|(T-g_{0}(X))^{i}-(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\bigr|\;\leq\;i(3\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i})^{i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

(111)

Proof
:

Write
\delta(X)\triangleq g_{0}(X)-\hat{g}(X)
so that

T-g_{0}(X)=\eta
and
T-\hat{g}(X)=\eta+\delta(X)
.
Binomial expansion gives

(\eta)^{i}-(\eta+\delta)^{i}\;=\;-\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\binom{i}{j}\,\eta^{\,j}\,\delta^{\,i-j}.

Taking absolute values, expectations, and using independence

(\eta\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X)
together with Hölder,

\mathbb{E}\bigl|(\eta)^{i}-(\eta+\delta)^{i}\bigr|\;\leq\;\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\binom{i}{j}\;\mathbb{E}|\eta|^{\,j}\;\mathbb{E}|\delta|^{\,i-j}\;\leq\;\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\binom{i}{j}\;\mathbb{E}|\eta|^{\,j}\;\varepsilon_{1}^{\,i-j}.

Since
\eta
is sub-Gaussian with
\|\eta\|_{\psi_{2}}=\psi_{\upeta}
, we have
\mathbb{E}|\eta|^{j}\leq(2\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{j})^{j}\leq(2\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i-1})^{j}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl|(\eta)^{i}-(\eta+\delta)^{i}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq{\epsilon}_{1}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\binom{i}{j}(2\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i-1})^{j}{\epsilon}_{1}^{i-1-j}\leq i{\epsilon}_{1}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\binom{i-1}{j}(2\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i-1})^{j}{\epsilon}_{1}^{i-1-j}

\displaystyle\leq i{\epsilon}_{1}(2\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i-1}+{\epsilon}_{1})^{i-1}\leq i(3\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i})^{i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

\square

Lemma G.8

(Identifiability guarantee)
.

We have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\right|\geq\frac{1}{2r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}.

Proof
:

By assumption (2) in
Theorem

5.4
and
Lemma

G.6
, we know that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\right|\geq\frac{1}{r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}},

By
Lemma

G.3
, we have
|a_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}
. Moreover, by
Lemma

G.7
, for
i\in[r+1]
it holds that

\mathbb{E}\left|(T-g_{0}(X))^{i}-(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\right|\leq i(3\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i})^{i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\quad\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\big(a_{ir}\mathbb{E}\big[(T-g_{0}(X))^{i}\big]-\hat{a}_{ir}\mathbb{E}\big[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\big]\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}|a_{ir}|\cdot\mathbb{E}\big|(T-g_{0}(X))^{i}-(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\big|+|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}|\cdot\big|\mathbb{E}\big[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\big]\big|

(112)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!}\big[(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}\cdot i(3\psi_{\upeta}\sqrt{i})^{i}{\epsilon}_{1}+(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1}\cdot 2^{2i}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{i}i^{i/2}\big]

(113)

\displaystyle\leq\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!}i^{1+i/2}

\displaystyle\leq 100\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{1},

where
Equation

112
follows from triangle inequality,
Equation

113
follows from
Lemma

G.3
,
Lemma

G.4
,
Lemma

G.7
and
Equation

68
.
From our assumption
Equation

22
and
Lemma

G.1
, we can deduce that this quantity is smaller than
\frac{1}{2r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}
, concluding the proof.

\square

Remark G.1
.

With a similar reasoning, one can also deduce that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|\leq 3\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{1}.

(114)

This inequality will be used later in the proof.

Lemma G.9

(Second-order moment bounds)
.

The following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{2}\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{2(r+1)}

(115)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 112(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})^{2}\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{2r}

(116)

Proof
:

Recall that

\hat{J}_{r}(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}a_{ir}w^{i-1},\quad|a_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{r+1-i}

(117)

by
Lemma

G.3
, and

\mathbb{E}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{r}\right]\leq 2^{2r}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{r}k^{k/2}

(118)

by
Equation

68
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}a_{ir}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!}\left(\frac{T-\hat{g}(X)}{8\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i}\right)^{2}\right]

(119)

\displaystyle=(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq l+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\mathbb{E}\left[\big((T-\hat{g}(X))/(8\psi_{\upeta})\big)^{i+j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq l+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}2^{2(i+j)}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i+j}(i+j)^{(i+j)/2}

(120)

\displaystyle\leq r^{2}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq l+1}\frac{1}{i!j!}2^{3(i+j)}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i+j}i^{i/2}j^{j/2}

(121)

\displaystyle\leq r^{2}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq l+1}2^{3(i+j)}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i+j}

(122)

\displaystyle=r^{2}(8\psi_{\upeta})^{2(r+1)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}8^{i}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i}\right]^{2}

(123)

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{2}\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{2(r+1)},

(124)

where
Equation

119
follows from
Equation

117
,
Equation

120
follows from
Equation

118
,
Equation

121
follows from
\left(\frac{i+j}{2}\right)^{i+j}\leq i^{i}j^{j}
which is a direct consequence of Jensen’s inequality,
Equation

104
follows from
i!\geq i^{i/2}
. This concludes the proof of
Equation

115
.

With a similar reasoning, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{4r}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(i-1)!}\left(\frac{T-\hat{g}(X)}{8\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i-1}\right)^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{4r}\sum_{0\leq i,j,u,v\leq l}\frac{1}{i!j!u!v!}\mathbb{E}\left[\big((T-\hat{g}(X))/(8\psi_{\upeta})\big)^{i+j+u+v}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{4r}\sum_{0\leq i,j,u,v\leq l}\frac{1}{i!j!u!v!}2^{2(i+j+u+v)}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i+j+u+v}(i+j+u+v)^{(i+j+u+v)/2}

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{4r}\sum_{0\leq i,j,u,v\leq l}\frac{1}{i!j!u!v!}2^{4(i+j+u+v)}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i+j+u+v}i^{i/2}j^{j/2}u^{u/2}v^{v/2}

\displaystyle\leq(8\psi_{\upeta})^{4r}\left[\sum_{i=0}^{r}16^{i}\left(1+\frac{C_{\mathsf{g}}}{\psi_{\upeta}}\right)^{i}\right]^{4}

\displaystyle\leq 2\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{4r}.

Since
\xi
is
\psi_{\upxi}
-sub-Gaussian, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{4}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\xi+\theta\eta+q(X)-\hat{q}(X))^{4}\big]

\displaystyle\leq 22\left(\mathbb{E}_{P}[\xi^{4}]+C_{\uptheta}^{4}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\eta^{4}]+\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(q(X)-\hat{q}(X))^{4}\big]\right)

\displaystyle\leq 22\big[4^{4}(\psi_{\upxi}^{4}+C_{\uptheta}^{4}\psi_{\upeta}^{4})+{\epsilon}_{2}^{4}\big],

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{4}\big]^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{4}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 112(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{2r}.

\square

By definition, we have for any
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
that

\displaystyle D_{q}D_{g}^{r}\mathbb{E}_{P}[m(Z,\theta,h_{0}(X))]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r+1}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\big]

\displaystyle D_{g}^{r+1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[m(Z,\theta,h_{0}(X))]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r}\theta\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\right].

Since
m(Z,\theta,h(X))
with
h=(g,q)
can be viewed as an
(l+2)
-th order polynomial of
g
and
q
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X))\big]\right|

(125)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[m(Z,\theta_{0},\hat{h}(X))\big]-\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\big]\right|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{k!}\Big(kD_{q}D_{g}^{k-1}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\cdot(\hat{q}(X)-q_{0}(X))(\hat{g}(X)-g_{0}(X))^{k-1}\bigg.

\displaystyle+\;\bigg.D_{g}^{r}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\cdot(\hat{g}(X)-g_{0}(X))^{r}\Big)\bigg]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\mathbb{E}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{k-1}(\eta)\right]\cdot(\hat{q}(X)-q_{0}(X))(\hat{g}(X)-g_{0}(X))^{k-1}\right|

\displaystyle+\;\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{k!}\mathbb{E}\left|\theta_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{k-1}(\eta)\right](\hat{g}(X)-g_{0}(X))^{r}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\frac{1}{k!}\Big(k(4e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r+1-k}{\epsilon}_{1}^{k-1}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}(4e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r+1-k}{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(4e)^{r}\left({\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}\right),

where in the last but one step we use the fact that
|\theta_{0}|\leq C_{\uptheta}
.
By
Equation

115
and Chebyshev inequality, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\bigg|

(126)

\displaystyle\leq 4(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 8(\gamma n)^{-1/2}r^{2}\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r+1}

and

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\hat{q}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\bigg|

(127)

\displaystyle\leq 4(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 50(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}

with probability
\geq 1-\gamma
.
Our assumption on
l
,
Lemma

G.8
, and
Equation

126
together imply that

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\bigg|\leq\frac{3}{4r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}},

(128)

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\bigg|\geq\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\Big|-\frac{3}{4r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}\geq\frac{1}{4r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}

It also directly follows from
Equation

127
that

\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\hat{q}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\bigg|\leq 51(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}.

By triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\quad\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-q_{0}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|+\mathbb{E}_{P}|\hat{q}(X)-q_{0}(X)||\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))|

\displaystyle\leq|\theta_{0}|\cdot\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|+\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[(\hat{q}(X)-q_{0}(X))^{2}\big]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\uptheta}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\Big|+\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+3\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\uptheta}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\Big|+\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}(3{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}),

where the penultimate inequality follows from
\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\big]\leq 3\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}{\epsilon}_{1}
which can be shown in a similar fashion as
Lemma

G.9
, and the final inequality follows from
Equation

114
. Hence we can deduce that

\displaystyle\quad\bigg|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\hat{q}(X_{i}))\hat{J}_{r}(T_{i}-\hat{g}(X_{i}))\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\Big|+50(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\uptheta}\kappa_{r+1}+\big[8(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}(3{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2})+50(\gamma n)^{-1/2}(\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta})\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}

(129)

\displaystyle\leq 6C_{\uptheta}\kappa_{r+1},

(130)

where
Equation

129
follows from
Lemma

G.6
and
Equation

130
uses the constraint on
r
given in
Equation

22
.
Recall that
\hat{\theta}
satisfies
\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=n/2+1}^{n}m(Z_{i},\theta,\hat{h}(X_{i}))=0
, so
Equation

128
and
Equation

130
together imply that

|\hat{\theta}|\leq 6C_{\uptheta}.

As a result,
Equation

126
and
Equation

127
yield

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\big[m(Z,\hat{\theta},\hat{h}(X))\big]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 64(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\big[16(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})\big]^{r}\big[r^{2}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})+\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta}\big]

Subtracting this inequality from
Equation

125
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|(\theta_{0}-\hat{\theta})\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 16^{r}\Big[{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+64(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{r}\big(r^{2}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})+\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta}\big)(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\Big].

Therefore, we conclude that

\big|\theta_{0}-\hat{\theta}\big|\leq r!16^{r}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\Big[{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+64(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{r}\big(r^{2}(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})+\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}\psi_{\upeta}\big)(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\Big].

G.2
Proof of
Theorem

5.3
:
Theorem 5.3

(ACE estimation error)
.

In this section, we outline how the proof of
Theorem

5.4
in the previous section can be slightly modified to obtain
Theorem

5.3
.

Lemma G.10

(Condition for bias domination)
.

Suppose

\Delta_{i}\;\triangleq\;\big|\kappa_{i}-\hat{\kappa}_{i}\big|\leq\underbrace{10(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{i}(i-1)!r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}}_{\text{variance}}+\underbrace{(2i{\epsilon})^{i}}_{\text{bias}},\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq r,

a\triangleq 2\log\big(C_{\mathsf{T}}{\epsilon}_{1}^{-1}/2\big)>0,

and
b\triangleq\log(\gamma n/100)>0
.
If

r\;\leq\;\frac{b}{\,a\,},

(131)

then for every
1\leq i\leq r

10(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{i}(i-1)!r^{1/2}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\;\leq\;(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{\,i},

i.e. the bias term
(2i{\epsilon}_{1})^{\,i}
dominates the variance term in

Lemma

G.10
.

Lemma G.11

(Bounding polynomial coefficients)
.

For any
i\in[r+1]
we have

|a_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}C_{\mathsf{T}}^{r+1-i}.

(132)

Proof
:

The proof is the same as that of
Lemma

G.3
.

\square

Lemma G.12

(Bounding the estimation error of polynomial coefficients)
.

For any
i\in[r+1]
we have

|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}|\leq\frac{1}{(i-1)!}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

(133)

Proof
:

The proof is the same as that of
Lemma

G.4
.

\square

Lemma G.13

(Key lemma; approximate orthogonality)
.

If
r
satisfies
Lemma

G.10
, then under
{\mathcal{E}}
(defined in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
in
Section

F.2
) we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{J}_{r}^{(k)}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]\leq(4e{\epsilon}_{1})^{r-k}.

Proof
:

The proof is the same as that of
Lemma

G.5
.

\square

Lemma G.14

(Linear–in–
\epsilon_{1}
moment difference)
.

Let

T=g_{0}(X)+\eta,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\eta]=0,\qquad\eta\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X,\qquad\|\eta\|_{\psi_{2}}=:\psi_{\eta}<\infty.

Assume an estimate
\hat{g}
satisfies

\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{L^{s}(P)}\leq{\epsilon}_{1}\leq\psi_{\upeta},\quad\text{for some }s\geq 2,

and fix an integer
1\leq i\leq s/2
.
Then

\mathbb{E}\bigl|(T-g_{0}(X))^{i}-(T-\hat{g}(X))^{i}\bigr|\;\leq\;i(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{i}{\epsilon}_{1}.

(134)

Proof
:

The proof is similar to
Lemma

G.7
; the only difference is that the moment bound of
\eta
becomes
\mathbb{E}|\eta|^{j}\leq C_{\mathsf{T}}^{j}
.

\square

Lemma G.15

(Identifiability guarantee)
.

We have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))\right]\right|\geq\frac{1}{2r!}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}.

Proof
:

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

G.8
, the left-hand-side can be shown to be
\leq 3(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r}{\epsilon}_{1}
. Combining with the constraint
Equation

20
yields the desired conclusion.

\square

Lemma G.16

(Second-order moment bounds)
.

The following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{2}(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{2(r+1)}

(135)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[(Y-\hat{q}(X))^{2}\hat{J}_{r}(T-\hat{g}(X))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 112C_{\mathsf{Y}}^{2}\big(2C_{\mathsf{T}}\big)^{2r}

(136)

Equipped with the above lemmas, we can then follow the arguments in
Section

G.1
to deduce that

\big|\theta_{0}-\hat{\theta}\big|\leq r!4^{r}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\Big[{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+64C_{\mathsf{T}}^{r}\big(r^{2}C_{\mathsf{T}}+C_{\mathsf{Y}}\big)(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\Big].

G.3
Special cases of the ACE estimator

When
r=3
,
Theorem

5.4
immediately implies the following result:

Corollary G.1

(Third-order ACE estimator)
.

Let
\delta_{\mathsf{id}}>0
and
C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{g}},C_{\mathsf{q}},\psi_{\upeta},\psi_{\upxi}\geq 1
be constants and
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
be the set of all distributions in
{\mathcal{P}}(C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{g}},C_{\mathsf{q}};\psi_{\upxi},\psi_{\upeta})
such that
\eta
is independent of
X
and
|\kappa_{4}|\geq\delta_{\mathsf{id}}
. Suppose that
\theta
is the solution to
Equation

61
with
m(Z,\theta,h(X))=\left[Y-q(X)-\theta\left(T-g(X)\right)\right]\left[(T-g(X))^{3}-3\mu_{2}^{\prime}(T-g(X))-(\mu_{3}^{\prime}-3\mu_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})\right]
. Then for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}
such that

\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\big[{\epsilon}_{1}^{3}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{4}+(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}+\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}C_{\upeta})(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{3}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\big].

The choice of the moment function in
Corollary

G.1
has also been proposed in
Mackey et al. 2018
, though their results are restricted to the high-dimensional linear regression setting. However, the rate that we derive from
Corollary

G.1
is faster than theirs, and as a consequence, in
Corollary

5.1
we need a weaker sparsity assumption to achieve
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
rate. The main insight for deriving this improved rate is that the moment function is, in fact, third-order orthogonal. By contrast
Mackey et al. 2018
only shows that it is second-order orthogonal. We will revisit this setting in
Section

6
, where we empirically verify the effectiveness of ACE for different choices of
r
.

For
r\geq 4
, to the best of our knowledge, the estimators derived from
Theorem

5.4
are new. For illustration purpose, we derive the guarantee for
r=4
in the following:

Corollary G.2

(Fourth-order ACE estimator)
.

Let
\delta_{\mathsf{id}}>0
and
C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{g}},C_{\mathsf{q}},\psi_{\upeta},\psi_{\upxi}\geq 1
be constants and
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
be the set of all distributions in
{\mathcal{P}}(C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{g}},C_{\mathsf{q}};\psi_{\upxi},\psi_{\upeta})
such that
\eta
is independent of
X
and
|\kappa_{5}|\geq\delta_{\mathsf{id}}
. Suppose that
\theta
is the solution to
Equation

61
with

\displaystyle m(Z,\theta,h(X))

\displaystyle=[Y-q(X)-\theta\left(T-g(X)\right)]\big[(T-g(X))^{4}-6\mu_{2}^{\prime}(T-g(X))^{2}

\displaystyle-4(\mu_{3}^{\prime}-3\mu_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})(T-g(X))-(\mu_{4}^{\prime}-6\mu_{2}^{\prime 2}-4\mu_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{3}^{\prime}+12\mu_{1}^{\prime 2}\mu_{2}^{\prime}-6\mu_{1}^{\prime 4})\big].

Then for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}
such that

\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\big[{\epsilon}_{1}^{4}{\epsilon}_{2}+C_{\uptheta}{\epsilon}_{1}^{5}+(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta}+\psi_{\upxi}+C_{\uptheta}C_{\upeta})(C_{\mathsf{g}}+\psi_{\upeta})^{4}(\gamma n)^{-1/2}\big].

Remark G.2

(Cumulants versus moments)
.

Generalizing the
r=3
case to
r\geq 4
is highly nontrivial. Indeed, given the construction in
Corollary

G.1
, one might be tempted to consider

m(Z,\theta,h(X))=\left[Y-q(X)-\theta\left(T-g(X)\right)\right]\left[(T-g(X))^{r}-r\mu_{l-1}^{\prime}(T-g(X))-(\mu_{r}^{\prime}-r\mu_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{r-1}^{\prime})\right]

with
h=(g,q)
. In this case, let
\Delta(x)=g(x)-\hat{g}(x)
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[D^{(0,1)}m(Z,\theta,h(X))\mid X\big]=-\mu_{r}+\mu_{r}^{\prime}-r\mu_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{r-1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}[\eta^{r}]+\mathbb{E}[(\eta+\Delta(X))^{r}\mid X]-r\mathbb{E}[\eta+\Delta(X)\mid X]\mathbb{E}[(\eta+\Delta(X))^{r-1}\mid X]+{\mathcal{O}}_{P}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{r-2}]\Delta(X)^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}_{P}(n^{-1/2})={\mathcal{O}}_{P}({\epsilon}_{1}^{2}+n^{-1/2}),

which is the same as the
r=3
case up to constants. As a result, this approach does not yield rates faster than
Corollary

G.1
.

In
Corollaries

G.1
and
G.2
, we omit the constants in the upper bounds. As shown in
Theorem

5.4
, the constants for
r
-th order orthogonal estimators can be at most
(Cr)^{r}
for some constant
C
, that grows super-exponentially, and, as demonstrated in
Remark

5.4
, the growth of this constant is in some cases offset by the growth of the absolute cumulant
|\kappa_{r+1}|
.

G.4
Proof of
Corollary

5.1
:
Corollary 5.1

(ACE estimation error for high-dimensional sparse linear regression)
.

As derived in
Mackey et al. 2018
, there exists some constant
C_{\gamma}^{\prime}>0
such that on an event
\mathcal{E}
with probability
\geq 1-\gamma/2
, the Lasso nuisance estimates simultaneously provide the bounds

\textstyle\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|_{2}\leq C_{\gamma}\sqrt{(s_{1}/n)\log p},\quad\|\hat{\beta}-\beta_{0}\|_{2}\leq C_{\gamma}\sqrt{(s_{2}/n)\log p},

(137)

where we recall that
\beta_{0},\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Since
X\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\bm{I}})
, Cauchy-Schwarz and Khintchine’s inequality
[
Vershynin 2018
, Corollary 2.6.4]
imply that, on this event,

\textstyle{\epsilon}_{1}

\textstyle=\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{L^{2r+2}(P_{X})}=\left\|\left\langle X,\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\right\rangle\right\|_{L^{2r+2}(P_{X})}

\textstyle\leq K\sqrt{2r+2}\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|_{2}\leq K\sqrt{(2r+2)(s_{1}/n)\log p}\quad\text{and, similarly,}\quad

(138)

\textstyle{\epsilon}_{2}

\textstyle=\|\hat{q}-q_{0}\|_{L^{2r+2}(P_{X})}\leq K\sqrt{(2r+2)(s_{2}/n)\log p},

for some universal constant
K>0
.
Our assumption that

\textstyle\max(s_{1},s_{1}^{r/(r+1)}s_{2}^{1/(r+1)})=o(n^{r/(r+1)}/\log p)

(139)

further implies that, on the event
\mathcal{E}
,
\max\{{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1},{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}\}=o(n^{-1/2})
. Applying
Theorem

5.4
with
\gamma
replaced by
\gamma/2
, we can therefore conclude that the advertised bound holds with probability
(1-\gamma/2)^{2}\geq 1-\gamma
.

Appendix H
Additional experiment results and discussion

(a)

The average MSE of ACE estimation with order
l=1,2,5
as functions of
\xi
.

(b)

The distribution of estimates for second- and fifth-order ACE estimation, with varying
\xi
.

Figure 2
:
The sensitivity of ACE estimators to correlation of the covariate
X
and the noise variable
\eta
.

(a)

The average MSE of ACE estimator with
l=1,2,\cdots,6
as functions of sample size.

(b)

The distribution of estimates for second-order ACE estimation when
n=10000,s=200
.

(c)

The distribution of estimates for fifth-order ACE estimation when
n=10000,s=200
.

Figure 3
:
Experiment results for ACE estimators with fixed sample size
n=10000
and varying sparsity.

In view of
Theorem

5.4
, the error rate of
r
-th order ACE estimator depends on a
bias
term that scales as
{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1})
and a
variance
term that scales as
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
, multiplied by a constant depending on
r
. In practice, this constant is often non-negligible. Hence, to choose an appropriate order
r
, one should take into consideration its effect on the final estimation error.

Varying sample size.
We first investigate the performance of ACE estimators with
r\leq 6
with varying sample size. The results are reported in
Figure

2
. We set
r=1
as a baseline and only plot the results of estimators that are better than
r=1
. From
Figure

1
one can see that the fifth order estimator performs the best, followed by the second order one. The fifth order estimator incurs large errors for small sample sizes (
n=2000
) but the error decreases rapidly when
n
grows larger.

When
n\geq 8000
, the decreasing rates of different estimators are roughly the same. This is because in this regime, variance becomes the dominating term in the total mean-squared error. From a theoretical perspective, this is because for fixed
s
, the LASSO estimates induce errors that scale as
n^{-1/2}
, so for any
r\geq 1
, the bias term becomes
n^{-(r+1)/2}\ll n^{1/2}
. In
Figure

1(d)
and
Figure

1(e)
we plot the distributions of the estimates produced by first and fifth order ACE estimators. One can see that the variances of these two estimators are roughly at the same level, while the first-order estimator induces significantly larger biases, especially when
n
is small.

In
Figure

1(f)
, we plot the
95\%
confidence intervals for each individual estimates. We observe that the actual percentage of confidence intervals covering the ground-truth parameter is
94.9\%
, quite close to what
Corollary

E.1
predicts.

Correlation between covariate and treatment noise.
The theoretical benefits of ACE estimators with
r\geq 3
crucially relies on the assumption that
X
and
\eta
are independent, which might be restrictive. However, it might be the case that they are weakly correlated,
i.e.
, only a small part of
\eta
is correlated with
X
. We would like to understand the sensitivity of our estimators’ performance with respect to such correlation.

Specifically, we assume that the treatment variable is drawn from

T=g_{0}(X)+(1+\xi X_{1})\eta,

(140)

where
X_{1}
is the first component of
X
and
\eta
is a mean-zero random variable independent of
X
. We set
p=100,n=20000,s=40
investigate the estimation error of ACE with different
r
’s as a function of the correlation coefficient
\xi
.

The results are reported in
Figure

2
, where we compare the top-
3
estimators among
r=1,2,\cdots,6
. The first- and second-order estimators have stable performance across different correlations, while the performance of the fifth-order one deteriorates rapidly when
\xi\geq 0.1
. This suggests that it would be better to use the second-order estimator unless one has strong prior knowledge that
X
and
\eta
are weakly correlated. In the context of pricing experiments, the data is drawn from a company’s historical experimentation records, so that scientists are likely to have knowledge about the high-level design principles of such experiments.

Varying sparsity.
Lastly, we investigate the relative performance of ACE estimators with different level of sparsity, for fixed
p=1000,n=10000
. Recall that sparsity affects the first-stage nuisance errors, which in turn affects the bias of our estimates.

The results are reported in
Figure

3
. From
Figure

3(a)
we can see that the performance of first-order estimator deteriorates rapidly when the support size grows. By contrast, the performances of second- and fifth-order estimators are quite stable, with the fifth-order one slightly better for smaller
s
. This is not surprising, since one can see from
Theorem

5.4
that the bias term for the fifth-order estimator has a larger bias, so it would only be smaller than the second-order counterpart when the nuisance errors are small enough. As shown in
Figures

3(b)
and
3(c)
, when
s=200
, the fifth-order estimator indeed incurs a larger bias.

Appendix I
More results for orthogonal machine learning

I.1
Construction of orthogonal moment functions

Theorem I.1

(Construction of higher-order orthogonal moments)
.

Let
a_{ik}(x)
and
\rho_{ik}(w)
be as defined in
Lemma

4.1
, then the following statements hold:

1.

If
\mathbb{E}\left[(1+|\eta|)|\rho_{ik}(\eta)|\mid X=x\right]<+\infty
holds for all
1\leq i\leq M_{r}
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the moment function

\displaystyle m_{r}\left(Z,\theta,q(X),g(X),\{a_{ik}(X)\}_{i=1}^{M_{r}}\right)=\big[Y-q(X)-\theta(T-g(X))\big]J_{r}(T-g(X),X)

with nuisance functions
h_{1}=g,h_{2}=q
and
h_{i+2}=a_{ik},i\in[M_{r}]

is
(S_{0},S_{1})
-orthogonal where
S_{0}
contains all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}
that satisfies at least one of the following conditions:

(i).
\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq 1
; (ii).
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\leq k
;
(iii).
\alpha_{4}=1
,
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
, the remaining
\alpha_{i}
’s equal zero;

and
S_{1}=\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}:\max\left\{\alpha_{2},\sum_{i=3}^{M_{r}+2}\alpha_{i}\right\}\geq 2\right\}
.
Moreover,
D^{\alpha}m
exists and is continuous for all
\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq k+1
.

2.

Let
C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{T}},C_{\mathsf{Y}}>0
be constants and
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\star}(C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{T}},C_{\mathsf{Y}})
. Let
\theta
be the solution of
Equation

61
with
m=m_{r}
. Then under
E.1
(2) and (3), for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>0
that only depends on
\gamma,C_{\uptheta},C_{\mathsf{T}},C_{\mathsf{Y}}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))

\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\bigg[\sqrt{\frac{V_{\mathsf{m}}}{n}}+k\lambda_{\star}({\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2})

(141)

\displaystyle+M_{r+1}\lambda_{\star}\big(({\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2})\tilde{{\epsilon}}+({\epsilon}_{1}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}){\epsilon}_{4}\big)\bigg].

holds with probability
\geq 1-\gamma
, where
\tilde{{\epsilon}}=\max_{i\neq 1,2,4}{\epsilon}_{i}
,
s\geq k+1
and

\lambda_{\star}=\sup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\max_{\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{r,\ell}\setminus S_{0})\cup({\mathcal{I}}_{r+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1})}\left\|D^{\alpha}m_{r}(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right\|_{L^{2}(P)}.

Theorem

I.1
is proven in
Section

I.2
. It shows that by successive integration of
J_{0}
, we can construct moment functions that are orthogonal with respect to
g_{0}(X)
and
q_{0}(X)
with arbitrarily high order, while being only first-order orthogonal with respect to the nuisances
a_{ik}(X)
. In (3), the constants
\delta_{\mathsf{id}}
and
V_{\mathsf{m}}
depend on the order
k
; we will write them as
\delta_{\mathsf{id},k}
and
V_{\mathsf{m},k}
to avoid confusion.

From another perspective,
Theorem

I.1
can be viewed as a special case of
Theorem

4.1
, because the construction of the moment function there ensures that
\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{M_{j}}a_{ij}(X)\rho_{ij}(T-g_{0}(X))\mid X=x\right]=0,j=1,2,\cdots,k
. In
Theorem

I.1
, the
a_{ij}(\cdot)
’s are viewed as nuisance functions which allows for
exact
orthogonalily properties. By contrast, in
Theorem

4.1
only
q(\cdot)
and
g(\cdot)
are treated as nuisance functions and we only ask for
approximate
orthogonality. While they look similar at first glance, an important observation is that this result does
not
rely on any explicit assumptions on the estimation errors of
\hat{a}_{ij}(\cdot)
’s. Indeed, it might be possible that the left-hand side of
Equation

15
is much smaller than the individual estimation errors of
\hat{a}_{ij}(\cdot)
’s, because these individual errors cancel out in the summation. This observation will prove helpful in
Section

5
.

Recall that in
Theorem

3.2
we show that higher-order orthogonality is impossible for the Gaussian treatment, even with known variance. In this case, since the distribution of
\eta=T-g_{0}(X)\mid X
is known, the functions
a_{ik}
’s are known as well. However, in the Gaussian case, any moment function constructed from
Equation

8
when
k\geq 2
would violate the identifiability assumption (
E.1
(3)). We prove this in
Proposition

I.2
.

I.2
Proof of
Theorem

I.1

In this subsection, we provide a straightforward instantiation of
Theorem

E.1
using the moment functions constructed in
Lemma

4.1
. For simplicity, we restrict ourselfs to the case where the treatment and outcome are both bounded.

Proof of (1).
The statement follows directly from induction. By assumption, the conclusion holds for
k=0
. Now assume that it holds for some
k-1\geq 0
, then

I_{r}(w,x)=\int_{0}^{w}J_{r-1}(w^{\prime},x)\textup{d}w^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{M_{r}}a_{ir}(x)\int_{0}^{w}\rho_{ir}(w^{\prime})\textup{d}w^{\prime},

so we can choose
M_{r}=M_{r-1}+1
,
a_{ir}(x)=a_{i-1,r-1}(x),\rho_{ir}(w)=\int_{0}^{w}\rho_{i-1,r-1}(w^{\prime})\textup{d}w^{\prime},2\leq i\leq M_{r}-1
and
a_{1,r}(x)=-\mathbb{E}[I_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X=x],\rho_{1,r}(w)\equiv w
, proving the result for
r
.

Proof of (2).
First note that

Y-q_{0}(X)-\theta_{0}(T-g_{0}(X))={\epsilon},

so that

\mathbb{E}\left[m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mathbb{E}[{\epsilon}\mid X,T]\right]=0.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{M_{r}+2}
with
\|\alpha\|_{1}=1
, we consider three cases,

•

\alpha_{1}=1
, then (
8
) implies that

\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=-\mathbb{E}\left[J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]=0.

•

\alpha_{2}=1
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]

\displaystyle=\theta_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[(Y-q_{0}(X)-\theta_{0}(T-g_{0}(X)))J_{r-1}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]=0.

•

\alpha_{i+2}=1
for some
i\geq 1
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(Y-q_{0}(X)-\theta_{0}(T-g_{0}(X)))\rho_{ir}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]=0.

Next, we consider
\alpha
’s of form
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},0,\cdots,0)
where
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\leq r
. Since
m
is affine in
q
, when
\alpha_{1}\geq 2
we have
D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))=0
, so we only need to consider the case when
\alpha_{1}\in\{0,1\}
. Similar to the arguments above,

•

If
\alpha_{1}=1,\alpha_{2}\leq r-1
then

\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=(-1)^{\alpha_{2}+1}\mathbb{E}\left[J_{r-\alpha_{2}}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]=0.

•

If
\alpha_{1}=0,\alpha_{2}\leq r
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=(-1)^{\alpha_{2}}\alpha_{2}\theta_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[J_{r+1-\alpha_{2}}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\left[(Y-q_{0}(X)-\theta_{0}(T-g_{0}(X)))J_{r-\alpha_{2}}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]=0.

Furthermore,

•

If
\alpha_{1}=1,\alpha_{4}=1,\rho_{4,r}(w)\equiv w
and the remaining
\alpha_{j}
’s are all zero, then

\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=-\mathbb{E}[T-g_{0}(X)]=0.

•

If
\alpha_{1}=1,\alpha_{4}=1,\rho_{4,r}(w)\equiv w
and the remaining
\alpha_{j}
’s are all zero, then

\mathbb{E}\left[D^{\alpha}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]=2\theta_{0}\mathbb{E}[T-g_{0}(X)]=0.

This proves the orthogonality properties related to
S_{0}
. Since
m(Z,\theta,\gamma)
is affine in
\gamma_{i},i\geq 2
(which corresponds to the nuisance functions
q
and
a_{ir},i\in[M_{r}]
), we have
D^{\alpha}m\equiv 0
as long as
\sum_{i\geq 2}\alpha_{i}\geq 2\Leftrightarrow\alpha\in S_{1}
. Hence
m
is
(S_{0},S_{1})
-orthogonal as desired.

Finally, the continuity of
D^{\alpha}m
is obvious since
m
is a quadratic function in terms of the nuisance functions.

Proof of (3).
By
Theorem

E.1
,

\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id}}^{-1}\times\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{V_{\mathsf{m}}}{n}}+\lambda_{\star}\sum_{\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{r,\ell}\setminus S_{0})\cup({\mathcal{I}}_{r+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1})}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\bigg).

(142)

As shown in part (2),
{\mathcal{I}}_{r,\ell}\setminus S_{0}
only contains
\alpha
with
\alpha_{2}\in\{0,1\},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\geq 1,\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}+\alpha_{4}\geq 3
and
\sum_{i\geq 3}\alpha_{i}=1
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in({\mathcal{I}}_{r,\ell}\setminus S_{0})}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{r+1}\frac{1}{j!}\left({\epsilon}_{1}^{j-2}{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{j-1}\right)\tilde{{\epsilon}}+({\epsilon}_{1}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}){\epsilon}_{3}\leq 3({\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2})\tilde{{\epsilon}}+({\epsilon}_{1}^{2}+{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}){\epsilon}_{3}.

On the other hand,
{\mathcal{I}}_{r+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1}
contains
\alpha
with
\alpha_{2}\leq 1
and
\sum_{i\geq 3}\alpha_{1}\leq 1
, so that

\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}_{r+1,\ell,0}\setminus S_{1}}\frac{1}{\|\alpha\|_{1}!}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{\epsilon}_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\leq\frac{1}{(r+1)!}\big[({\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+{\epsilon}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2})+({\epsilon}_{1}^{r}+{\epsilon}^{r-1}{\epsilon}_{2})\tilde{{\epsilon}}\big]

Combining the above two inequalities, the conclusion follows.

I.3
Example: heteroscedastic nonparametric regression

In general,
a_{ik}(\cdot)
may be hard to estimate since it is a linear combination of conditional moment functions. Generally speaking, there is no guarantee that estimating these conditional moment functions is easier than estimating the nuisance functions.

In this section, we revisit the nonparametric regression problem with heteroscedastic noise, where fast rates for estimating
a_{ik}
’s are indeed achievable. Specifically, suppose that the treatment variable is sampled from the regression model

T=g_{0}(X)+\eta,

where the noise variable
\eta=V_{0}(X)^{1/2}\eta^{\star}
and
\eta^{\star}
satisfies
|\eta^{\star}|\leq C_{\eta^{\star}}
a.s.,
\mathbb{E}[\eta^{\star}\mid X]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[{\eta^{\star}}^{2}\mid X]=1
. For this problem, the following result is known from
Wang et al. 2008
.

Proposition I.1
.

[
Wang et al. 2008
, Theorems 1,2 and Remark 3]

Assuming that
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1]
and
g_{0}(\cdot),V_{0}(\cdot)
are
\alpha
and
\beta
-th order smooth respectively, then given i.i.d. data
\{(X_{i},T_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
from some distribution such that the marginal density of
X
exists and is bounded away from
0
, there exists an estimator
\hat{V}(\cdot)
that achieves the optimal mean-square error rate
\|\hat{V}(X)-V_{0}(X)\|_{P,2}={\mathcal{O}}_{P}\left(n^{-\min\left\{2\alpha,\beta/(2\beta+1)\right\}}\right)
.

In particular, when
\beta>\alpha
, one can in fact estimate
V_{0}(\cdot)
with higher accuracy than estimating
g_{0}(\cdot)
. In this subsection, we additionally assume that the distribution of
\eta^{\star}
is known and let
\mu_{r}^{\star}(x)=\mathbb{E}[{\eta^{\star}}^{r}\mid X=x]
be its
r
-th moment. Thus we can estimate
\mu_{r}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\eta^{r}\mid x=x]=V_{0}(x)^{r/2}\mu_{r}^{\star}(x)
with
\hat{V}(x)^{r/2}\mu_{r}^{\star}(x)
. We conjecture that by using a similar approach as
Wang et al. 2008
one can directly construct higher-order moment estimates for unknown
\eta^{\star}
, so the assumption that
\eta^{\star}
is known can be removed.

To state our main result for this setting, we assume that
g_{0}
and
V_{0}
are
\alpha
and
\beta
-th order smooth respectively:

Assumption I.1
.

g\in\Lambda^{\alpha}(M_{g})
and
V\in\Lambda^{\beta}(M_{V})
for some constants
M_{g},M_{V}>0
and
\alpha,\beta>0
.

Let
\hat{g}
be an optimal estimate of
g_{0}
under the
L^{\infty}
-norm, that achieve the rate
{\epsilon}_{1}={\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\log n/n\right)^{\alpha/(2\alpha+1)}\right)

[
Stone 1982
]
. Also let
\hat{V}
be the estimate of
V_{0}
in
Wang et al. 2008
that achieves the
L^{2}
-rate

{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}={\mathcal{O}}\big(n^{-\min\{4\alpha,2\beta/(2\beta+1)\}}\big).

Consider the moment function
m_{r}
defined in
Equation

10
. Its nuisance functions
a_{ik}(x),i=0,1,\cdots,k
are functions of the conditional moments of
\eta\mid X
. Then we can derive their estimates
\hat{a}_{ik}(x)
by directly plugging in the variance estimates:
\hat{\mu}_{r}(x)=\hat{V}(x)^{r/2}\mu_{r}^{\star}(x)
. The next theorem provides theoretical guarantee for the resulting estimate derived from this approach:

Theorem I.2

(Error rate for heteroscedastic nonparametric regression)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}=[0,1]
,
C_{\mathsf{T}}\geq 1
be a real number and
{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of all distributions in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}
that satisfy
I.1
and
|T|\leq C_{\mathsf{T}}
. Let
\hat{g},\hat{V}
be defined above and
\hat{q}
be some estimator of
q_{0}
such that
\|\hat{q}-q_{0}\|_{L^{2}(P)}\leq{\epsilon}_{2}
. Also assume that
|\hat{\mu}_{r}(x)|\leq(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r}
4
4

4

This is without loss of generality, since otherwise we can replace
\hat{\mu}_{r}
with
\min\{(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r},\max\{-(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r},\hat{\mu}_{r}\}\}
. By assumption we know that
|\mu_{r}|\leq(2C_{\mathsf{T}})^{r}
, so this would only reduce the estimation error.
. Then the following statements hold:

1.

Consider the nuisance functions and their estimates specified in the previous paragraph. Then for all
i\in[r+1]
, we have

\left\|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}\right\|_{L^{2}(P)}\leq\big(8eC_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{r+1-i}\frac{r+1-i}{i!}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\leq A_{r}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}},

where
A_{r}=r\big(8eC_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{r+1}
.

2.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}}
be the solution to
Equation

61
with the moment function
m=m_{r}
defined in
Equation

10
. Then in the setting of
Theorem

I.1
, for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))\leq C_{\gamma}\delta_{\mathsf{id},r}^{-1}\bigg[\sqrt{\frac{V_{\mathsf{m},r}}{n}}+r\lambda_{\star}\big({\epsilon}_{1}^{r+1}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{r}{\epsilon}_{2}+A_{r}({\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}){\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\big)\bigg].

(143)

Theorem

I.2
is proven in
Section

I.4.1
. Note that
Theorem

I.2
makes structural assumptions on
g_{0},V_{0}
but not on
q_{0}
. As a result, the assumption is stronger than the fully structure-agnostic setting, while being weaker than the Holder-smoothness setting. Even in this interpolated regime, to the best of our knowledge, there is no existing results that achieve faster rates than DML.

It is worth noticing that the rate
{\epsilon}_{v}
can be faster than
{\epsilon}_{1}
to arbitrary order. Thus, there exists an optimal
k
that balances the dependency on
n
and the magnitude of the constants
A_{r},\delta_{\mathsf{id},r},V_{\mathsf{m},r},\lambda_{\star}
. For
r=2,3
, assuming that these constants are uniformly bounded and that
q
also belongs to a Holder class, we can derive the following result:

Corollary I.1
.

In the setting of
Theorem

I.2
, if we additionally assume that
q\in\Lambda^{\gamma}(M_{q})
and
\|\hat{q}-q_{0}\|_{L^{2}(P_{X})}\leq{\epsilon}_{2}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-2\gamma/(2\gamma+1)})
, then the following holds:

1.

Let
r=2
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))={\mathcal{O}}_{P}(n^{-1/2})
as long as
\min\{\alpha,\gamma\}>\frac{1}{4}
and
\min\{\alpha,\gamma\}\beta>\frac{1}{4}
.

2.

Let
r=3
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{n,1-\gamma}(\hat{\theta}_{\textsc{OML}};{\mathcal{P}}_{s,{\epsilon}}(\hat{h}))={\mathcal{O}}_{P}(n^{-1/2})
as long as
\min\{\alpha,\gamma\}>\frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{4}
and
\min\{\alpha,\gamma\}\beta>\frac{1}{4}
.

Corollary

I.1
implies that
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}(n^{-1/2})
rate can be achieved under weaker smoothness requirements if one uses third-order orthogonal estimators. Its proof can be found in
Section

I.5
.

I.4
Technical details in
Section

I.3

I.4.1
Proof of
Theorem

I.2

Proof of part (1).
By definition, we have

\hat{a}_{ir}(x)=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(r+1-i)!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}(x),

(144)

while
\hat{\kappa}_{i}
’s are cumulant estimates obtained by directly plugging in
\hat{V}(\cdot)
:

\hat{\kappa}_{i}=\hat{\mu}_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\binom{i-1}{j-1}\hat{\mu}_{i-j}\hat{\kappa}_{j},\quad\hat{\mu}_{1}=0,\,\hat{\mu}_{i}=\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[\hat{V}(X)^{r/2}\mu_{r}^{\star}(X)\big](i\geq 2).

By the mean value theorem, there exists some
\tilde{V}(\cdot)
between
\hat{V}
and
V_{0}
such that
\hat{V}^{j/2}(x)-V_{0}^{j/2}(x)=\frac{j}{2}\tilde{V}^{j/2-1}(x)(\hat{V}(x)-V_{0}(x))
. Hence

\displaystyle\|\hat{V}^{j/2}-V_{0}^{j/2}\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle=\frac{j}{2}\big\|\tilde{V}^{j/2-1}(x)(\hat{V}(x)-V_{0}(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{j}{2}\big\|\tilde{V}^{j/2-1}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(P)}\cdot\big\|\hat{V}-V_{0}\big\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{j}{2}C_{\mathsf{v}}^{j/2-1}\big\|V-\hat{V}_{0}\big\|_{L^{2}(P)},

where we recall that
C_{\mathsf{v}}
is the assumed uniform upper bound on
|V_{0}(X)|
and
|\hat{V}(x)|
.
We can then bound the estimation error of
\hat{\mu}_{r}(\cdot)
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mu}_{r}(X)-\mu_{r}(X)\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\big(\hat{V}(X)^{r/2}-V_{0}(X)^{r/2}\big)\mu_{r}^{\star}(X)\big\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq rC_{\mathsf{v}}^{r/2-1}C_{\upeta^{\star}}^{r}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}.

Via a similar reasoning as in
Lemma

F.1
, we can deduce by induction that

|\hat{\kappa}_{j}(X)|\leq\big(2jC_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{j},\quad\|\hat{\kappa}_{j}(X)-\kappa_{j}(X)\|_{L^{2}(P)}\leq\big((2j+1)C_{T}+1\big)^{j}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\leq\big(4jC_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{j},

which then implies an upper bound on the error of
\hat{a}_{ik}(\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\|a_{ir}-\hat{a}_{ir}\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{1}{(r+1-i)!i!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\bigg(\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(X)-\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}(X)\bigg)\bigg\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(r+1-i)!i!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\bigg\|\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(X)-\prod_{B\in\pi}\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}(X)\bigg\|_{L^{2}(P)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(r+1-i)!i!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\sum_{B\in\pi}\big\|\kappa_{|B|}-\hat{\kappa}_{|B|}\big\|_{L^{2}(P)}\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi\setminus\{B\}}\max\big\{\big|\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}(x)\big|,\big|\hat{\kappa}_{|B^{\prime}|}(x)\big|\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(r+1-i)!i!}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{r+1-i}}\sum_{B\in\pi}\big(4|B|C_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{|B|}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\pi\setminus\{B\}}2\big(2|B|C_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{|B|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(r+1-i)!i!}2\big(2C_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}4^{j}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j})\in\mathrm{P}_{r+1-i,j}}\binom{r+1-i}{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}}\prod_{s=1}^{j}i_{s}^{i_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{i!}\big(2C_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{r+1-i}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1-i}4^{j}e^{r+1-i}p(r+1-i,j)

\displaystyle\leq\big(8eC_{\mathsf{v}}^{1/2}C_{\upeta^{\star}}\big)^{r+1-i}\frac{r+1-i}{i!}{\epsilon}_{\mathsf{v}},

where we follow the arguments employed in
Lemma

G.4
.

Proof of part (2).
This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

I.1
and part (1).

I.5
Proof of
Corollary

I.1

Let
\rho=\min\{\alpha,\gamma\}>\frac{1}{4}
, then we have
\|\hat{g}-g_{0}\|_{P_{0},\infty},\|\hat{q}-q_{0}\|_{P_{0},\infty}=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}_{P}(n^{-\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}})
, so that
{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}_{P}(n^{-\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}})=o_{P}(n^{-1/6})
.

Also
Proposition

I.1
implies that

\max\{{\epsilon}_{4},\tilde{{\epsilon}}\}={\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-\min\left\{2\alpha,\beta/(2\beta+1)\right\}}\right).

It remains to show that

\displaystyle\max\{{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\}\cdot\max\{{\epsilon}_{4},\tilde{{\epsilon}}\}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})\Leftarrow\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}+\min\Big\{2\alpha,\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}\Big\}>\frac{1}{2}.

Since
2\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, it remains to check that

\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}+\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}>\frac{1}{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\beta\rho>\frac{1}{4}

which holds by assumption. This proves (1).

To prove (2), we use a similar argument except that the upper bound becomes

|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}|={\mathcal{O}}_{P}\big({\epsilon}_{1}^{4}+{\epsilon}_{1}^{3}{\epsilon}_{2}+\max\{{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\}({\epsilon}_{4}^{2}+\tilde{{\epsilon}}).

Since
\rho\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{4}>\frac{1}{6}
, we have
\max\{{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\}^{4}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-\frac{4\rho}{2\rho+1}})={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1/2})
. Moreover, the assumption guarantees that

\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}+\min\Big\{2\alpha,\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}\Big\}\geq\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}+\min\Big\{2\rho,\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}\Big\}\geq\frac{1}{2}

since
\frac{\sqrt{3}-1}{4}
is the positive root of the equation
\frac{\rho}{2\rho+1}+2\rho=\frac{1}{2}
. This concludes the proof.

I.6
Comparison with
Mackey et al. 2018

In
Mackey et al. 2018
the authors consider polynomial-based moment functions. These constructions can be derived from our
Theorem

I.1
by choosing
J_{1}(w,x)=w^{k}-\mu_{k}(x)
where
k
is some positive integer and
\mu_{k}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\eta^{k}\mid X=x]
. For
r=2,3
, we obtain the following special cases:

Example I.1
.

By choosing
r=2
, we recover the moment function

\displaystyle m\big(Z,\theta,q(X),g(X),\{\mu_{i}(X)\}_{i=k}^{k+1}\big)

(145)

\displaystyle=\big[Y-q(X)-\theta(T-g(X))\big]\big[(T-g(X))^{k+1}-\mu_{k+1}(X)-(k+1)(T-g(X))\mu_{k}(X)\big]

proposed by
Mackey et al. 2018
. Thus,
Theorem

I.1
implies that this moment function satisfies all conditions in
E.1
with the orthogonality set
S=\{\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq 2\}\setminus\{(1,0,0,1),(0,1,0,1)\}
.

Example I.2
.

By choosing
r=3
, we obtain

\displaystyle m\big(Z,\theta,q(X),g(X),\mu_{2}(X),\{\mu_{i}(X)\}_{i=k}^{k+2}\big)

(146)

\displaystyle=\big[Y-q(X)-\theta(T-g(X))\big]\times\big[(T-g(X))^{k+2}-\mu_{k+2}(X)-(k+2)(T-g(X))\mu_{k+1}(X)

\displaystyle-(k+1)(k+2)\big((T-g(X))^{2}-\mu_{2}(X)\big)\mu_{k}(X)/2\big]

with nuisance functions
h(X)=(q(X),g(X),\mu_{2}(X),\mu_{k}(X),\mu_{k+1}(X),\mu_{k+2}(X))\in\mathbb{R}^{6}

is orthogonal with respect to
S=\{(a,b,0,0,0,0)\mid a+b=3\}\cup\{\alpha\mid\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq 2\}\setminus\{(a,b,c,d,0,e)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{6}\mid a+b=c+d+e=1\}
.

I.7
More discussion on
E.1

The following result states that if the distribution of
\eta\mid X=x
does not depend on
x
and its density has certain good properties, then
E.1
would not be violated with
J_{2}
, unless
\eta
is Gaussian.

Proposition I.2

(Identifiability v.s. orthogonality)
.

The moment function
m
in
Theorem

I.1
(2) satisfies
E.1
if and only if

\mathbb{E}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]\neq 0.

Moreover, the following statements hold:

1.

If
\eta\mid X=x
is Gaussian for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]=0,\forall r\geq 2
.

2.

If
\eta\mid X=x
is non-Gaussian with twice continuously differentiable density
p(\cdot)
that does not depend on
x
, then there exists
J_{1}(w,x)
in the form of (
7
), such that
\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{3}(T-g_{0}(X),X)]\neq 0
and
\mathbb{E}[(1+|\eta|)|\rho_{i2}(\eta)|]<+\infty,\forall 1\leq i\leq M_{2}
.

3.

Suppose that
\eta\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X
and
\eta
is non-Gaussian, and let
\{J_{r}\}_{r=1}^{+\infty}
be the sequence generated from
J_{1}(w,x)=w
(assuming that all of them are well-defined). Then there exists
r\geq 2
such that
\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)]\neq 0
.

The first part of
Proposition

I.2
can be directly derived from the Stein’s Lemma, while the second part is derived from a characterization of the solutions property of the Gauss-Airy’s equation
xy-ay^{\prime}-by^{\prime\prime}=0.

[
Durugo 2014
,
Ansari 2016
]

Generalizing this result to
k\geq 3
requires characterizing the solution properties of higher-order Gauss-Airy’s equation, which we leave for future work.

Proof
:

(1) is straightforward from Stein’s lemma.
To prove (2), orthogonality implies that

0=\mathbb{E}\left[D_{q}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\mid X\right]=-\mathbb{E}\left[J_{3}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X\right],

(147)

and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[D_{qg}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\mid X\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[J_{3}^{\prime}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X\right],

(148a)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[D_{qgg}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\mid X\right]=-\mathbb{E}\left[J_{3}^{\prime\prime}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X\right],

(148b)

where we slightly abuse notation and use
J_{3}^{\prime},J_{3}^{\prime\prime}
to represent the partial derivatives with respect to the first argument.

Note that
m
is partially linear in
\theta
, we have

m(Z,\theta,h_{0}(X))=\theta\cdot\nabla_{\theta}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))+(Y-q_{0}(X))J_{3}(T-g_{0}(X),X),

so (2) and (3) are both equivalent to

\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla_{\theta}m(Z,\theta_{0},h_{0}(X))\right]\neq 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbb{E}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{3}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\right]\neq 0.

(149)

We argue that there must exist some
J_{3}(w,x)
that is polynomial in
w
, such that the equations (
147
), (
148
) and (
149
) hold simultaneously. Since by assumption, the density
p(\eta)
of
\eta=T-g_{0}(X)\mid X
does not depend on
X
, (
148a
) is equivalent to

0=\int J_{3}^{\prime}(w,x)p(w)\textup{d}w=-\int J_{3}(w,x)p^{\prime}(w)\textup{d}w\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\int J_{3}(w,x)p^{\prime}(w)\textup{d}w=0,

and similarly, (
148b
) is equivalent to

\int J_{3}(w,x)p^{\prime\prime}(w)\textup{d}w=0.

Lemma

I.1
at the end of this subsection implies that in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
wp(w)\notin\mathrm{span}\langle p^{\prime}(w),p^{\prime\prime}(w)\rangle
, so by
Lemma

I.2
, there must exists some function
\tilde{J}_{3}(w)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\int\tilde{J}_{3}(w)p^{\prime}(w)\textup{d}w=\int\tilde{J}_{3}(w)p^{\prime\prime}(w)\textup{d}w=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int\tilde{J}_{3}(w)wp(w)\textup{d}w>0.

(150)

Moreover, we can assume WLOG that
\int\tilde{J}_{3}(w)p(w)\textup{d}w=0
, since replacing
\tilde{J}_{3}
with
\tilde{J}_{2}-c_{0},\forall c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
does not affect the properties in (
150
). We define
\tilde{J}_{2}(w)=\tilde{J}_{3}^{\prime}(w)
and
\tilde{J}_{1}(w)=\tilde{J}_{3}^{\prime\prime}(w)
. In the following, we show that
J_{1}(w,x)=\tilde{J}_{1}(w)
satisfies all the desired properties. First, since
\tilde{J}_{3}
is a polynomial, so is
\tilde{J}_{1}
. Second, by (
148b
) we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{J}_{1}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{J}_{3}^{\prime\prime}(T-g_{0}(X),X)\mid X\right]=0.

Finally, recall that
p(\cdot)
is the probability density function of
\eta=T-g_{0}(X)
, so

\mathbb{E}\left[(T-g_{0}(X))\tilde{J}_{3}(T-g_{0}(X))\right]=\int\tilde{J}_{3}(w)wp(w)\textup{d}w>0,

concluding the proof of (2).

Finally, under the conditions of (3),
Lemma

G.6
implies that
\mathbb{E}[(T-g_{0}(X))J_{r}(T-g_{0}(X),X)]=\frac{1}{r!}\kappa_{r+1}
, where
\kappa_{i}
is the
i
-th order cumulant of
\eta
. We know from Levy’s Inversion Formula
[
Durrett 2019
, Theorem 3.3.11]
that non-Gaussian distributions must have at least one non-zero cumulant, so the conclusion immediately follows.

\square

Lemma I.1

(Solution of second-order Airy equation)
.

Let
p(\cdot)
be the probability density function of a random variable
\eta
that is second-order continuous differentiable, and that

xp(x)+2ap^{\prime}(x)+bp^{\prime\prime}(x)=0

(151)

for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, then we must have
b=0,a>0
and thus
\eta
must be Gaussian.

Proof
:

Since
p(\cdot)
is a density function, the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma implies that its Fourier transform
\hat{p}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}p(x)\textup{d}x
must vanish at infinity. On the other hand, applying Fourier transform to both sides of (
151
) yields

i\hat{p}^{\prime}(\xi)+2ai\xi\hat{p}(\xi)-b\xi^{2}\hat{p}(\xi)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{p}(\xi)=Ce^{-a\xi^{2}-ib\xi^{3}/3}.

Thus we must have
a>0
. If
b\neq 0
,
[
Ansari 2016
, 4.2]
then implies that there exists constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
p(x)
has the same sign as the Airy function
\mathrm{Ai}(c_{1}x+c_{2})
. However, it is well-known that
\mathrm{Ai}(x)
can take both positive and negative values, which is a contradiction.

\square

Lemma I.2

(Separability of inner products)
.

Suppose that
f_{i}(w),i=0,1,2
are continuous functions
such that
f_{0}\notin\mathrm{span}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle
. Then there exists a function
J(w)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\int\left|J(w)f_{i}(w)\right|\textup{d}w<+\infty,\quad i=0,1,2

and

\int J(w)f_{i}(w)\textup{d}w=0,i=1,2\quad\text{and}\quad\int J(w)f_{0}(w)\textup{d}w>0.

Proof
:

Suppose that such
J(w)
does not exist.
For any finite interval
[a,b]
and a sequence of
{\mathcal{C}}^{3}
functions
S=\left\{g_{j},j=1,2,\cdots,n\right\}
supported on
[a,b]
(we denote the set of such functions by
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{3}([a,b])
), we define the vector
u_{i}^{S}=\left(\int g_{r}(w)f_{i}(w)\textup{d}w\right)_{k=0}^{+\infty},i=0,1,2
. Then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}
, by choosing
J(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(w)
, our assumption implies that

\lambda^{\top}u_{1}^{S}=\lambda^{\top}u_{2}^{S}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lambda^{\top}u_{0}^{S}=0.

Let
v_{0}^{S}
be the orthogonal projection of
u_{0}^{S}
onto
\mathrm{span}\langle u_{1}^{S},u_{2}^{S}\rangle
and
\lambda=u_{0}^{S}-v_{0}^{S}
, then
\lambda^{\top}u_{1}^{S}=\lambda^{\top}u_{2}^{S}=0
and
\lambda^{\top}u_{0}^{S}=\|\lambda\|^{2}
, so that
\lambda=0
and
u_{0}^{S}\in\mathrm{span}\langle u_{1}^{S},u_{2}^{S}\rangle
.

We consider the following two cases:

1.

For any finite
S
,
\mathrm{rank}\left(\mathrm{span}\langle u_{1}^{S},u_{2}^{S}\rangle\right)\leq 1
, then
u_{0}^{S}
is parallel to
u_{1}^{S}
for all
S
. Since
u_{1}\neq 0
, it is easy to show that
u_{0}=\alpha u_{1}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. As a result, we have
\int J(w)(f_{0}(w)-\alpha f_{1}(w))\textup{d}w=0
for any
J(\cdot)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{3}([a,b])
, so we must have
f_{0}-\alpha f_{1}\equiv 0
on
[a,b]
.

2.

There exists some finite
S
such that
\mathrm{rank}\mathrm{span}\langle u_{1}^{S},u_{2}^{S}\rangle=2
, then there exists unique
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
u_{0}^{S}=\alpha u_{1}^{S}+\beta u_{2}^{S}
. By considering any set
S_{1}=S\cup\{s\},s\notin S
, one can show that
u_{0}^{S_{1}}=\alpha u_{1}^{S_{1}}+\beta u_{2}^{S_{1}}
as well, so
u_{0}^{S}=\alpha u_{1}^{S}+\beta u_{2}^{S}
for any finite set
S
. As a result, we have
\int J(w)(f_{0}(w)-\alpha f_{1}(w)-\beta f_{2}(w))\textup{d}w=0
for any
J(\cdot)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{3}([a,b])
, so we must have
f_{0}-\alpha f_{1}-\beta f_{2}\equiv 0
on
[a,b]
.

Now we have shown that for any interval
[a,b]
, there exists
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{0}-\alpha f_{1}-\beta f_{2}\equiv 0
on
[a,b]
. It is easy to derive from this fact that
f_{0}-\alpha f_{1}-\beta f_{2}\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}
,
i.e.
,
f_{0}\in\mathrm{span}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle
, which is a contradiction. Hence the conclusion follows.

\square
